BREAKING NEWS: Trump, Mark Zuckerberg, Dario Amodei, And More Discuss New AI Standards Accords

Forbes(2.2M)·37:58·1.3K views·2026-09-29
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Mark ZuckerbergCEO, Meta Dario AmodeiCEO, Anthropic
TL;DRPresident Trump and top tech leaders announced the formal signing of the "White House Accord on Super Intelligence," officially rebranding AI to SI (super intelligence) and committing to voluntary internal controls, multi-layered audits, and community partnerships to maintain U.S.

TL;DR

President Trump and top tech leaders announced the formal signing of the "White House Accord on Super Intelligence," officially rebranding AI to SI (super intelligence) and committing to voluntary internal controls, multi-layered audits, and community partnerships to maintain U.S. dominance over China while driving historic economic growth.

The White House Accord on Super Intelligence

  • Official Rebranding: President Trump announced that the industry officially changed the name from AI (artificial intelligence) to SI (super intelligence), declaring the former term a "fake word."
  • Voluntary Commitments:
    • White House Accord on Super Intelligence: Signed by top tech leaders and the President, establishing a joint commitment on frontier SI responsibilities and industry standards.
    • Moral and Industry Binding: President Trump described the accord as "morally binding" and comparable to a constitution for the industry.
  • Governance and Oversight:
    • Robust Internal Controls: Companies agreed to build internal systems for testing and risk detection.
    • Multi-Layered Auditing: Mark Zuckerberg outlined a review structure starting with internal risk reviews, followed by external auditors and evaluators.
    • Board Review: Company boards of directors will independently review all reports coming from external auditors.
    • Proposed Oversight Committee: The administration is considering forming a committee of about 10 people—potentially drawn from the industry group—to watch over the entire enterprise.

Economic Growth, Infrastructure, and Community Impact

  • Unprecedented Infrastructure Buildout: Dario Amodei noted the industry is undergoing the single largest infrastructure buildout in human history, creating chip plants, computer plants, and SI factories.
  • Job Creation: The sector is generating a million jobs in the United States, backed by several hundred billion dollars in venture capital and thousands of new companies.
  • Community Partnerships and Benefits:
    • Financial Contributions: President Trump and Speaker Mike Johnson emphasized that tech companies will form partnerships with local communities hosting data centers, offering financial generosity, school support, and teacher pay bonuses.
    • Local Investments: In Richland Parish, Louisiana, Meta is paying local teachers over $50,000 a piece in bonuses.
    • Legislative Backing: The House passed the Ratepayer Protection Act three weeks prior with almost no dissenting votes, codifying President Trump's pledge that tech companies will produce their own energy and put power back onto the grid.
  • Global Competitiveness: President Trump asserted that the U.S. is leading China and Europe significantly, warning that restrictive regulations would push data centers overseas to countries like Finland and China.

Safety, Innovation, and National Security

  • Safety and Innovation Coexistence:
    • Speaker Johnson stated that safety and innovation are not mutually exclusive pursuits.
    • Jensen Huang (NVIDIA) emphasized that there is no conflict between innovation, technology, and safety, noting the industry is actively developing new technologies to advance both capabilities and safety.
  • Self and Peer Policing: President Trump and tech executives expressed confidence in industry self-policing, arguing that high-IQ innovators and corporate stakes will prevent catastrophic failures.
  • National Security Priority: Congressional and administration officials stressed that winning the SI race against global competitors like China is the foremost priority for U.S. national security.

The Future of Work and the Age of Abundance

  • Age of Abundance: Elon Musk predicted that super intelligence and robotics will usher in an "age of abundance," resulting not just in universal basic income, but "universal high income."
  • Advanced Medical Care: Musk noted that super intelligence will provide everyone in the world with access to superior medical care.
  • Evolution of Roles: Musk argued that jobs will naturally evolve, comparing the shift away from manual calculations to past technological transitions, while noting that current labor demand remains exceptionally high.

Notable Quotes

"We have to strike the right balance here... the balance between the interest of the American people and benefiting from the extraordinary advancements that are being made with this new technology with the necessity of maintaining our edge over China." — Speaker Mike Johnson
"It's almost like a constitution in a way... And the biggest and the biggest people in the world signed that and I signed it as president and it really is a form of protection." — President Donald J. Trump
"There's no conflict between innovation, technology, and safety... We are going to advance this technology that is so consequential to humanity, so consequential to the future prosperity of our nation. And we're going to do it responsibly and safely." — Jensen Huang
"By far the most likely outcome is an age of abundance where we don't have just universal basic income, we have universal high income." — Elon Musk

I mean, I can summarize what I think we all signed today, which is the White House accord on developing this technology safely. The basic idea is that we want to give the American people and our customers confidence that the technology works in the way that we intend. Right? So, every company is building something that's a little bit different. Having one set of standards for what everyone should want to build—people want to do some different things, but I think what we can all agree on is that the American public and our customers want to know that what we're building is working in the way that we all intend. So we drafted a set of principles and commitments around building robust internal controls and detecting if there are any issues with the technology coupled with multiple layers of auditing and controls starting with internal risk review, external auditors and evaluators, and agreeing that we're going to have all of our boards of directors independently review the reports that come from the auditors. I think that these are good steps forward. The idea isn't that this is the only thing that we will ever do. Bill Ackman: People are Going to Lose a Lot of Money

The Knowledge Project Podcast(452.0K)·77:27·2026-09-29
Bill AckmanCEO, Pershing Square Capital Management
TL;DRBill Ackman breaks down his investment philosophy, the future of AI and market bubbles, and how a family medical emergency inspired him to build a world-class brain institute.
He also discusses scaling Pershing Square, transforming Howard Hughes into a modern Berkshire Hathaway,...

TL;DR

Bill Ackman breaks down his investment philosophy, the future of AI and market bubbles, and how a family medical emergency inspired him to build a world-class brain institute. He also discusses scaling Pershing Square, transforming Howard Hughes into a modern Berkshire Hathaway, and shifting away from traditional activism toward friendly dialogue and large-scale permanent capital.

Personal Crisis & The Brain Institute

  • Daughter's Medical Emergency: Bill Ackman’s daughter, Lucy, suffered a sudden brain hemorrhage due to an undiagnosed arteriovenous malformation (AVM) that caused 19 hours of brain pressure.
  • Resilience and Recovery: Despite grim initial prognoses, Lucy has made remarkable progress in regaining cognition, speech, and mobility through round-the-clock family support, friends visiting daily, and aggressive rehabilitation.
  • Ackman-Oaksman Institute (AIOI):
    • Ackman and his wife Neri closed on a vacant 3.4-acre biotechnology building at 65th and 11th Avenue 60 days after discovering it, intending to build a world-class brain institute focused on rehab, recovery, and longevity.
    • The institute aims to solve systemic inadequacies in hospital nutrition, short insurance-funded rehab windows, and medical nihilism surrounding brain injury recovery.
    • Leveraging technology and AI (including future bionic vision glasses and AI speech therapy) to make advanced neurological recovery accessible to all patients.

Investment Process & Public Markets

  • Research Methodology: A two-person team conducts deep dives starting with SEC filings (10-Ks, 10-Qs), earnings transcripts, expert networks, and competitor analysis, followed by an independent peer review before committing capital.
  • Criteria for "Super Durable" Companies: Focuses on simple, predictable, high free-cash-flow businesses with wide moats, strong balance sheets, and low risk of disruption.
  • The Checklist & Risk Management: Following a major loss in 2015–2016, Ackman engraved investment principles on stone tablets and instituted strict checklists, an aversion to short selling, and a focus on asymmetric macro bets (e.g., predicting COVID-19 shutdowns and inflation).
  • Netflix Case Study: Pershing Square initially bought Netflix on a subscriber dip, sold three weeks later when management abruptly shifted strategy to an ad-supported model (breaking the certainty thesis), and rebought the stock years later when it was cut in half after Netflix successfully won the streaming wars and executed on ads.

AI, Bubbles, and the Venture Landscape

  • Assessing AI Disruption: AI represents the fastest industrial revolution and pace of improvement ever seen, dramatically increasing the risk of disruption for dominant businesses.
  • AI Use Cases: Ackman views AI as a powerful research tool (not used for financial modeling to avoid groupthink) and used it to help vet medical decisions during his daughter's trauma.
  • Venture Capital Bubbles: Points to extreme private market froth—such as Series A rounds commanding $5B pre-money valuations and companies raising $50M at $400M valuations only to jump to $1B weeks later—driven by FOMO.
  • Advice to Founders: Raise capital now while available, but treat every dollar as your own and maintain years of runway rather than burning cash expecting perpetual liquidity; a high-profile blow-up will eventually reset the market.

Building a Modern-Day Berkshire Hathaway

  • Howard Hughes Transformation: Transforming Howard Hughes from a real estate developer of master-planned communities (MPCs like The Woodlands in Houston and communities in Texas, Las Vegas, and Hawaii) into a conglomerate.
  • The Berkshire Playbook:
    • Modeled after Warren Buffett, Ackman and CIO Ryan are shifting cash generated from land sales ($300M+ NOI and $4B in Hawaii condos under contract) into a growing P&C specialty insurance and reinsurance business (Bantage Holdings).
    • Pershing Square manages the assets of the insurance float while the company maintains a permanent capital base with zero stock dilution.
  • Ownership Alignment: Ackman owns ~47% of Howard Hughes, ensuring a long-term, unpressured strategic vision.

Activism, Leadership, and Public Life

  • Shift Away from Activism: Pershing Square has not made an activist investment since 2016; Ackman believes he no longer needs to run proxy contests because 3 million Twitter followers and a strong long-term track record grant sufficient influence to dialogue directly with CEOs.
  • Criteria for CEO Success: Prefers leaders who have "done it before" (e.g., recruiting Brian Nicols to Chipotle) and assesses candidates based on passion, high energy, capability, and unyielding character.
  • Personal Reflections: Reaches age 60 with a perspective that every bad experience yields a silver lining, viewing his career skills as providentially aligned to save his daughter and build a transformative brain institute.

Notable Quotes

"The what does not kill you makes you stronger thing is it is definitely true. And by the way, for my child, for Lucy, I think this is going to be good for her... if you can recover from something as devastating as this, it's going to make her into like a superhuman person." — Bill Ackman
"The thing that you need to think through is the risk of disruption and what AI has done is massively increased the risk of disruption... Businesses that seem like the most dominant businesses in the world, there will be some that will just disappear." — Bill Ackman
"We were almost never forced to make decisions by circumstances. So we always had options." — Charlie Munger (as recounted by Bill Ackman regarding Berkshire Hathaway's success)
Warren Buffett, the greatest investor of all time, was not able to perceive the risks of disruption created by the internet. Well, now we have AI. We're in the beginning of an industrial revolution. It's a much more complicated problem. If you had to guess, how do you think it plays out over the next 18 months? The pace of improvement is by far the fastest of anything we've ever seen. There will come a time, there will be some form of a blow-up where people lose a whole bunch of money. What's your investment process? Like, how do you go from "I'm interested in this company" to "now we're writing a check for a billion dollars"? Over time, you develop a spidey sense on people. If you look at our most successful investments, they've usually been cases where we've done something that someone hasn't done before. What advice do you have for the ordinary person who is making a paycheck and wants to invest? So, if you want to be an investor, you've had a challenging year this year with Lucy. What happened? You know, otherwise healthy child, what happened was she had, unbeknownst to us, what's called an arteriovenous malformation. It is a kind of structure in the brain where, the way the doctors describe it is, the arteries—you know, typically blood flows into the arteries, and then to the capillaries, and then to the veins. And when you go from arteries where there's a huge amount of blood flow to capillaries, the blood flow slows down, and then to the veins. In her case, she had what they call a bridge; it went from the artery directly to the vein. So over time, there was too much pressure going into these veins in her brain, and one of those veins burst. And then a large amount of blood filled her brain, which is a confined volume, and so it's a bit like a balloon blowing up inside your brain. That put pressure on the surrounding brain and the skull, and obviously, the skull doesn't move. So the pressure goes downward, it puts pressure on the midbrain. The midbrain is what keeps you breathing and keeps your heart beating. The really unfortunate thing—we believe this happened around 6:00 in the morning based on her Oura ring. She lived alone in Williamsburg, and I texted her that day. I had called her another time that day; other family members had done the same. But only when she hadn't picked up her luggage—she was leaving for Abu Dhabi for a wedding. A friend of hers was getting married the following day, and she was picking up her luggage from her mom's house. When she was two hours late and no one could reach her, my oldest daughter went to find her. They found her lying on the floor of her apartment, barely breathing, face down on the right side. And she thought she was dead. She called 911; she could hear her breathing in a very labored way, and then I don't sleep next to my cell phone. My daughter doesn't know the landline number for my house. So, she was able to call someone in the building; a guy in the building knocked on the door and pounded on the door. He handed me the phone with my daughter on it, and I hopped in a cab. The EMT didn't know which hospital to take her to because they didn't know what had happened to her. One of the big problems sometimes is that the EMT thinks someone's just drunk, and that's why they fell. And the way you treat a drunk person is you just let time go by. Right? Time going by for a person with a brain hemorrhage is very dangerous; the more time the brain is under pressure, the more damage that can take place. Got to the hospital at 9:00, and she was just sitting on a gurney there while they were trying to figure out what she had. Eventually, they gave her a CT, they figured out she had a hemorrhage, they called the surgeon, and what you need to do is release the pressure on the brain as quickly as possible. The way you do that is you remove about 40% of the skull to allow the brain to expand beyond the skull. We moved her from Elmhurst Hospital—by the way, I'm very pleased with how they cared for her there, City Hospital in Queens—to Mount Sinai where Neri and I spent some time helping, so we knew the team there. And they also have very good neurosurgeons and a neuroscience brain practice. She was on a breathing tube; she was wearing some bandages on her head, and it said, "no bone," right, to make sure that the nurses knew to be careful. And that's how we started. So, I basically gave the "dare to be great" speech. I said, "Let's see what can be achieved by whatever you need from us, unlimited resources. Let's look at the latest and greatest technologies." They basically told us, "Look, Bill, we don't know what kind of deficit she's going to have." You know, 19 hours of pressure on the brain—I didn't learn actually until weeks later—they don't normally do the surgery to save someone when it's been more than 5 hours because the assumption is brain death, basically. So 19 hours, she was as close to death as you can get. We didn't know what would become of her, but I'm an optimistic person. And she's regained over time her cognition. She's the same Lucy we knew; she's got the same sense of humor. She's really remarkable. She woke up not being able to walk, to speak, and unfortunately to see. Her walking is coming back; I think she's going to regain her ability to walk. Her speech, very slowly, is coming back. There's something like 17 vowels beyond the vowels that we were taught, and she can do about 15 of the 17 now. Each day she's almost learning a new consonant. I've spent a lot of time working with the doctors, and my pitch to them was: "Let's see what we can do for her, and everything we learn for her, we can help others." Neri and I are going to make this available to everyone. Three months after, I guess, I was coming back from my birthday party, and I got a call from a friend. We were talking to Mount Sinai actually about doing a redevelopment of their 5th Avenue campus to build a brain institute, some kind of brain center, and we were very aligned with our idea. Problem with that is it's probably a 10-year project and super complicated, obviously, taking down the existing hospital, moving people, bringing people back. And a vacant biotechnology building became available on 65th and 11th Avenue, 10 blocks from the office. 60 days later, we closed on the building; we signed a contract to buy the rest of the site. It's a 3.4-acre site. Our goal is to build the world's greatest brain institute, everything from a focus on the patient to: "How do we get treatments to patients?" There's a ton of interesting things going on in technology. Obviously, everyone knows Neuralink, but there are probably a dozen other companies working on brain-computer interfaces and other ways to get data from the brain. And, of course, AI. Not only are we able to get more and more data from the brain, we're able to interpret that data better than ever before. So, I'm hoping Lucy can speak normally on her own, just working at it. I'm hoping her vision returns. But if she has deficits in those areas, there will be solutions in the relative short-to-intermediate term. In terms of vision, a world where you wear a pair of glasses that basically have a camera, and the camera takes in the vision and ports it to your visual cortex—that's how you see. Elon says he's going to have a rudimentary way for people to see within a relatively short amount of time, within five years. Actually, I reached out to pretty much everyone for help on this, and he told me, he said five years. Well, people will have better vision—they will have bionic vision, in effect. The way Dr. Kellner describes it to me is that this has been Lucy's lead on this whole thing. A lot of neurologists, and even neurosurgeons, are nihilists. They don't believe that the patients can make really material recoveries, but they can. And we've heard a lot of interesting stories of recovery, which are great to share with Lucy, but we're going to be able to do a lot more. New York is actually probably one of the best places to do this, and there isn't anything like it. So, we're excited about it. What you guys have done in response is just incredible. Yeah, what's interesting is that all of the skills, experiences, relationships, and resources that I've developed over my career set me up to be incredibly well-positioned to help my daughter and to help other people that have this problem. I'm a real estate investor, right? So I was able to buy the building at a fraction of what it cost. And architecture and design—I'm also married to a very talented architect, who's going to play a pretty important role here. I think we're going to do something really interesting, and we've had an incredible outpouring of people who want to be part of this. In many cases, we love people who write to us who've dealt with this on a very personal level—have a brother, a sister, a mother, or a parent who has suffered, and they've seen the nature of the treatment that's available today, and they want to change that. It's interesting; actually, people say, "Bill, how do you deal with something like this?" Well, the first thing I had to do is figure out how to help my child. And as I made progress with that, this other thing is amazing. It makes me feel like something good is coming out of it. I have this view that from every bad thing, something good comes. This is among the good that's coming from what she's going through, and that makes me feel good. Did you always have that view or was that learned? I figured that out every time. When was there a moment when that... well, you know, I've had a few near-death experiences in business, and in each one of those cases, I was materially better off after that, so those are kind of good lessons. The "what does not kill you makes you stronger" thing is definitely true. And by the way, for my child, for Lucy, I think this is going to be good for her. Look, she's a wonderful, amazing person, but if you can recover from something as devastating as this, it's going to make her into a superhuman person. She is a wonderful human being. One of the things that's helping her recover is that her mom has been there every day. I moved my office into the hospital within a day of her moving in. I did two IPOs—the two Pershing IPOs—I did from room 107 on the 11th floor of 11 West at Mount Sinai Hospital, and power to Zoom. I would do a Zoom meeting for 45 minutes, then I would go spend 15 minutes with my daughter, and then I'd do the next meeting. I had my daughter as the inspiration for these two IPOs that we were working on. We only moved her out of the hospital August 8th, and she's been rehabilitating in Bridgehampton. Then she's coming back into the city for a little procedure, and then she's moving into her own apartment. We bought an apartment in our building within a few weeks of the incident, and we've been designing it to be the best rehab recovery place for her to live. Social interaction is absolutely critical for someone recovering from something like this. The emotional support that you get, and everything—her friends have been amazing. Her friends have been with her every day; one or more of her friends shows up and talks to her, eats with her, and engages with her. She's like this super giving person; she has a ton of friends, and everyone loves Lucy. She's getting an amazing return on that because her friends are showing up to save her. All of this makes me feel that humans can be so amazing. There's a lot more hope for people who've had strokes, brain injuries, or military TBI-type stuff, and there are a number of people in the field who have been really frustrated with how far we've not gone, who want to be the ones that fix this. What's been preventing us from going further? Why haven't we pushed the limits on this stuff? The answer is some combination of the insurance industry, the economics, and hospitals. A neurosurgeon wants to do their surgery and then move on to the next one. Neurosurgeons are highly compensated, but they get paid for doing surgeries; they don't get paid for the recovery of the patient over time. Every hospital has some kind of rehabilitation program, but in many cases, it's embarrassingly inadequate. The insurance company might pay for only six weeks of care, and then they get sent home, and their home is not the ideal place. It takes a village. What if they're the principal breadwinner and they're devastated by this? Maybe the other family member has to go out and work as opposed to caring for them. One of the most depressing things I was told by the head of rehab at Mass General—we've been talking to him—he said, "Bill, what Lucy's achieved is remarkable, she's having incredible care. The typical person wouldn't do the surgery. If they do the surgery, the typical patient ends up in a nursing home when they check out of the hospital because the family can't take care of them, and they die a few months later from pneumonia because of the care that can be achieved in a nursing home." It's super depressing. This is one of the cases where I am financially unconstrained in my ability to help her, so we can maximize the care. Now, what we're going to try to figure out in the institute is how we do this in a way that everyone can have the benefit of the care, and technology's going to help a lot. A great speech therapist could be $500 an hour; insurance will pay for whatever percentage of that, but only for some period of time. But people can keep recovering years and years later. AI could be an amazing speech therapist. There are lots of ways that technology is going to enable us to help people recover from these injuries. We're calling it the focus on brain rehab, recovery, and longevity. What does a brain rehab recovery institute do? It focuses on brain recovery, and your physical recovery. The brain plus your physical recovery is fundamentally longevity. The other thing that hospitals aren't very good at is nutrition; the food in hospitals is frightening. They have someone who has a heart attack, and the next morning they're having pancakes with syrup and orange juice. In Lucy's case, we made food for her for every meal. The institute's going to have amazing food because nutrition is critical. There are a lot of things we can do; taking advantage of this financial circumstance, we're going to use it in a way that will enable us to build something optimal and ultimately self-sustaining. You're an incredible father; what an amazing response to terrible circumstances. I would feel like one of the most frustrated people in the world if I couldn't do it. I literally feel like I was designed to help her, like everything I know how to do. You mentioned the Oura ring; what was the data that indicated something was wrong? I've been in touch with the CEO of Oura; he actually reached out on Twitter. I think you can, certainly with an Apple Watch and hopefully with an Oura ring. What happened was, if you look at her spike—I actually put it on Twitter—at 6:00 or so, there was a very quick spike in her pulse and then a drop-off that looked unusual. If you combine that spike and a drop-off with someone falling, like the Apple Watch has an alert when someone falls, you can reasonably assume: "Did they have a heart attack? Did they have whatever?" I think Oura ring should have the same capability and they're working on it. How do you keep your mind in a healthy place? What do you do to keep your head right? I play tennis almost every morning or do some form of exercise. I think that's probably the most important thing I do because it's the one thing I do in the course of the day where I'm completely focused on the ball that's coming at me, as opposed to whatever else is going on. That's a really important form of meditation for me. You have to get a good night's sleep, and I spend time with my family. Do you still meditate? I haven't in a while. I did for this challenging year, when I was in the middle of divorce and on my own, and it really helped. I probably should find 20 minutes a day to meditate. How do you manage it all? You have four effective public entities, you have this with your daughter, you have a 7-year-old, you want to be a great husband—how do you harmonize all these things? Pershing Square's come a long way from when I started it. I was sort of the chief bottle-washer guy, coming up with all the ideas, and I'd have an analyst who would help me prosecute them. Over 22 years, we've built an amazing team. We're in an industry where the top people can move from one place to another and get huge $100 million bonuses, and the result is a lot of turnover. We have no turnover at Pershing Square. If you look at the investment team, it has been the same for nine years, with a couple of new additions. That's a really good thing. If you have a constant revolving door of new people, you don't really know that you can trust them. When you've built a relationship with people for nine years and you have this culture of transparency and candor, I don't for a second ever question anything that's being told to me by a member of the investment team. That's a very comfortable place. The Pershing investment process is incredibly well-run by a very experienced team that I play an important role in, but I'm no longer the guy who generates the bulk of the ideas. The business itself is structured so that Ben is responsible for running a lot of the business elements, which frees me up to think, to work on strategic stuff, and to come up with the occasional idea. Pershing kind of takes care of itself while we have a number of public entities, and we have an amazing accounting team. There's the management company, Pershing Square Inc., and two funds that are publicly traded that own effectively the same portfolio. Each of them has independent boards of directors, and our role there is the external investment manager. We have excellent boards at both of those. The typical closed-end fund board is a bit of an embarrassment; I don't know if you follow the space, but I think I read somewhere that the same six directors sit on 86 closed-end fund boards. It's hard to do real governance when you sit on 86 boards. We hired actual real directors, we pay them like real directors, and they aren't serving on 50 other boards. What we do is fundamentally simple. Pershing kind of runs itself because of delegation, incentives, and alignment. Everyone is paid on individual stocks based on performance, and that compounding is what grows our fee stream. I own about 45% of the company, the team owns another 35%, and the rest is held by the public and strategic investors. If you get the alignment right and you have principles written on a stone tablet, it works. You talked about index funds there for a second. What advice do you have for the ordinary person who is making a paycheck and wants to invest? If you want to be an investor, it is something you have to allocate real time to. It is something you have to study and learn. You have to do your homework on companies, so that is a decision. If you just want to have exposure in the stock market, I do think index funds are a very good approach, and they have beaten most active investors over long periods of time. How would you do that? Would you do dollar cost averaging on a monthly basis? Would you be like, "Oh, it's high now, so I'll hold off"? How do you think about it? I think the key is to start young. The sooner you can start putting aside money that you can invest for the long term, the better for your retirement. You know, the power of compounding. I wouldn't sit around holding cash because you think the market's expensive. You mentioned stock options earlier. I want to come back to that. How do you think about stock options from a mature company perspective, like Meta or Microsoft? In an example like that where they could pay cash easily but they use options or they use restricted stock as some form, the benefit of restricted stock or options is the vesting. It gives you some ability to retain talent in a way that just paying someone cash every year doesn't. So, I think it is still a very useful tool. It also obviously creates more alignment; people actually care about the share price, which I think is an important discipline for employees. Now, at Pershing Square, the management company, we don't intend to issue any meaningful amount of options or restricted stock. We have an employee base that already owns it; we spread equity very widely throughout the firm. So, 80% of the stock or so is held by the team. We are in the fortunate position of not having to issue equity or options for a very long time. But maybe 20 years from now, if we have a new generation that doesn't have any equity in the company, we may choose to use it as it can be a useful tool. Oh, I was very happy the market gave me an opportunity to purchase some of that when it came out. It went down to like $23 or something. Yeah, I bought some, too. Okay, so I want to talk about Netflix for a second. You bought this, you sold it like a month later, and then now you have rebought it. What went into that decision? You were wrong, now you are right. How did you change your mind? Netflix is one of the companies, if you will, in the library that we had done a lot of work on over time. What created the opportunity, at least the first time as we thought about it, was that they had missed subscriber growth guidance and the stock got crushed. We think Netflix is an amazing business with a very dominant position, so we bought a meaningful stake in the company. We then met with management, we shared our detailed deck with them, and they completely agreed with our thesis on the company. So, we felt we were super aligned with management. We talked about things like why Netflix didn't have an advertising model, a lower cost advertising model. They said, "We're never going to do it." About three or four weeks later, they announced earnings. If you go back and review the earnings call, management seemed shell-shocked. They missed the subscriber numbers again and they seemed very surprised by this. They talked about having to adapt and launching an advertising model, literally three weeks after they said they would never do it. My takeaway was: you make an investment in a company and then you learn new information that is inconsistent with the original thesis. You either have to buy a lot more because the stock got cheaper and you believe the new information is not material, or you have to exit because the thesis is broken. We made the decision to exit based on the thesis being broken. In the letter we wrote to our investors, we said, "Look, we're selling for this reason. We think management is going to be able to work through this problem, but Pershing Square invests in the highest certainty companies in the world, and we think the dispersion of outcomes here has widened dramatically." They might get it right and it might be a home run, but there is also a much greater probability that they don't get it right. They didn't know anything about advertising models and whether that would be successful. With the wide dispersion of outcomes, we had a better place to deploy the capital. We took the money and bought Google, Alphabet, because for us, we don't need to make it back the same way we lost it. We can always take a tax loss, which has value to us, and redeploy it in something else which is higher certainty. If you look at the Pershing Square portfolio, we own the highest certainty, predictable companies in the world. Netflix lost its certainty element, at least in our mind. Now what has happened since? They executed extremely well. They built a very successful advertising model, they became an even more dominant company, and they became a much more cash-flow generative business. The streaming wars? They won. The Disneys and the Paramounts of the world have kind of been pushed aside. Netflix—you probably turn off the lights practically before you turn off your Netflix. They are incredibly well-positioned to innovate. They are the place you go with any creative person to sell or produce your content. But because they executed really well, the stock went back to a very high multiple and wasn't interesting. Then, relatively recently, the stock got cut in half again. So now we had the high certainty business we thought we owned, they proved themselves, at a price that made sense. We bought it back. The business that you are involved with that I think people know the least about is Howard Hughes. How did you get involved in that? Why are you so excited by it? Howard Hughes was an entity we created to make another investment successful. We invested in a company called General Growth. We bought the stock during the financial crisis when it was down 99.5%. We bought 25% of the company a few months before it filed for Chapter 11. We were actually pushing the board to file for Chapter 11. They were trying to avoid it, so it was a case where it was inevitable. Our view was we could run a restructuring where the shareholders could keep their investment, maybe with some dilution. I joined the board of the company and we led that restructuring. What created complexity in General Growth versus its direct competitor, Simon Properties, which is still quite successful today, was that in addition to Class A shopping malls, they owned a lot of land. They did a lot of development and a business they acquired from Rouse; they owned these so-called MPCs, or small cities. The market hated that business. We said, "Let's make General Growth look exactly like Simon by taking all those businesses—all these land plays and MPCs—out of the company, and anything else that didn't look like a Class A mall." We took all the non-core stuff and stuck it in Howard Hughes. Normally, when you spin off a company, the parent company's stock price drops by the value of the thing you spin off because it is like a dividend. In this case, we spun off Howard Hughes, and General Growth stock went up. David Simon called it "shitco." It was a deal we did with Brookfield. He was making fun of it, but it was really sort of "shitco" because it was just everything that the assets nobody wanted. Then we hired a very good entrepreneurial team, David Weinerman and Greg Herlists, to work through these assets over time until the portfolio was quite focused in the last five or six years on just MPCs. We said, "The market is finally going to understand why this is a really good business." MPCs are master-planned communities; they are small cities at this point. We own the Woodlands in Houston, which is a small city with 150,000 people, high-rise office towers, shopping centers, schools, and churches. I describe the business like SimCity, where we act as the benign owner of these communities. What is in it for us is that we own all the commercial land and all the residential land. We sell the residential land to homebuilders, and we don't sell the commercial land; we use it to build whatever the community needs. If you take a long, multi-decade view, this is a business that over time generates a huge amount of cash. We have a series of these small cities. After 14 years of Wall Street wanting nothing to do with the business, we said it was time for us to transform Howard Hughes into something else. The reason Wall Street doesn't like the core Howard Hughes business is that land and development have a bad long-term track record. While our land is very different—imagine you owned New York City and you owned all the vacant commercial and residential land over a 100-year period—you would make trillions of dollars. That is the opportunity. The land we own is in places where people are moving: Texas, Las Vegas, Hawaii. Still, as a public stock, it has always traded with a big discount to the value of its assets. So over time, we have bought a significant stake. We now own 47% of the company, and we are transforming it into what we call a modern-day Berkshire Hathaway. What did Buffett do? He started with a dying textile operation. Over time, he liquidated the operation and reinvested the capital in insurance, banking, a candy company, and other businesses. He built a conglomerate that compounded capital at a very high rate over a long period, without issuing much stock. Since I became executive chair and Ryan became their CIO, we acquired an insurance company called Bantage Holdings, a specialty insurer and reinsurer. Most recently, we recruited what I believe is the best management team in the insurance industry. We are going to build this little insurer into a big one over time, with that very talented team underwriting the risk. Pershing Square is managing the assets. What Buffett did that was unique was running an insurance operation. In the beginning, he didn't do a very good job, but he learned. He managed the assets not in a portfolio of fixed-income securities, but by taking the float generated from writing insurance and putting it into short-term Treasuries so capital was available to pay claims. The balance he put into common stocks. When you earn an attractive return on assets and have negative-cost liabilities, you can run an insurer that generates a 20% or more annual rate of return. That is what we are underway to do at Howard Hughes. No one notices, why? Because it is still a real estate company that people hate. But the nature of Howard Hughes's core business is that over time, it itself liquidates. Each year, we sell hundreds of millions of dollars of land; over time, our land assets will go away. Each year, we sell hundreds of millions of dollars of condominiums in Hawaii. We have $4 billion of condominiums under contract. We generate something approaching $300 million of net operating income from the real estate assets. Historically, we took all the cash and reinvested it in real estate—we bought another MPC in Phoenix, for example. We are no longer going to do that. We are going to reinvest whatever capital is necessary to make these small cities amazing places to live, but beyond that, we are going to generate billions of capital to deploy initially in insurance. We have already put $300 million of additional capital in Bantage since we bought it. We are looking at things to accelerate the transformation from a real estate company to an insurance holding company. Buffett had a big stake; he owned half, and we own 47%. That allowed him to think long-term, so we are taking the long-term approach. We have the liability side set up to do smart things in insurance, and we are going to do a good job managing the assets. I have so many questions about this. So, "modern-day Berkshire Hathaway"—that term gets thrown around a lot. What does that mean to you? It means we are going to operate much the same way Buffett did. The driver of the value of Berkshire Hathaway over time is its insurance operation. When Buffett talked about buying Coca-Cola or American Express, those were assets purchased in the insurance company. We are going to take a long-term view on the way we manage this insurance operation, and we are going to grow the business without issuing a lot of common stock. There are a finite number of shares outstanding, and the beauty of insurance is that it is a very cash-generative business. Over the next several years, you will see the business transform. Today, it is probably 70% real estate and 30% insurance by capital. It will migrate over the next five years to maybe 75% insurance and 25% real estate, unless we figure out a way to do that more quickly. If you were to list the keys to Berkshire's success looking back, he had control, so he could take a long-term view. He wasn't exposed to the short-term whims of his shareholders. He had permanent capital. He didn't pay dividends. He retained all the capital the business generated. He had a talent for investing in common stocks, he recruited talented people, and he didn't dilute shareholders by issuing stock options or stock in acquisitions. Why don't more people copy that? It sounds so simple. It is not sexy to be in the investment operation of an insurance company; I think that is a big part of why people don't do it. Also, it is difficult to get to a 47% stake in a public company; that usually doesn't happen. This is a creature of history. Do you think a lot of Berkshire's success boils down to the fact that Buffett could have been paid 2 and 20, but he took a modest salary and grew his wealth alongside shareholders? I think his willingness to work for free, in effect, was helpful to the ethos around Berkshire. It probably made negotiations around compensation for other employees easier. Buffett ran a partnership where he got 25% of the profits over a 6% return. By the time he retired from that, I think he had $100 million under management. He realized that if he kept running in that format, he didn't have permanent capital, and he was tired of dealing with investors whose money affected his results. He did the math and said, "If I own half of this Berkshire Hathaway thing and I generate a 20% return, I'll end up in a good place." The marginal promote wasn't important to him. He was able to run the operation as a one-man band, which also enabled this. I remember talking to Charlie Munger one night and I asked him the most underrated aspect of Berkshire's success. He said, "We were almost never forced to make decisions by circumstances." So, we always had options. How do you think about that? What is your reaction? We designed our business around that. The unique thing about Pershing Square is that we manage an offshore entity, Pershing Square Holdings, where employees own 28%. We manage Howard Hughes, which is owned directly and indirectly by employees plus the Pershing Square funds. We just took public Pershing Square USA, and employees invested $500 million in the entity. We have these anchor stakes in each of the public vehicles we run, and each is a permanent capital entity. If there is a panic in the market and people want liquidity, they can sell the stock of these companies, but the capital stays in the vehicle, which enabled us to buy stock during the COVID crash or the financial crisis. Buffett decided at a certain age he didn't want to live a life dealing with constantly raising money, which is what you have to do in the hedge fund business. In the hedge fund business, when you do really well, institutional allocators take money away because you become too big a percentage of their portfolio. When you have a bad period, people take money away because you are having a bad period. You really can't close, because if you are not open, people don't do due diligence on you, and it becomes a multi-month process to learn about your business again. You spend all your time raising capital. As you get bigger, it consumes a huge amount of time. I attribute our biggest investment mistake to being distracted by having to be on the road raising capital to keep the base stable. We made a decision to get out of managing money where the money could leave. If we compound at close to historic rates, we will be managing a trillion dollars in 20 years, and that is a good business. Let's talk about Bremont. How did you get involved with the watch company? I was at a Howard Hughes board meeting in Dallas, and our office was in a complex with a shopping center. During a lunch break, I walked by a watch store, a salesman talked to me about a Bremont, and I bought a watch. I eventually lost it in a watch safe. Years later, I was heading to London. A friend told me he was wearing a Patek Philippe and it was stolen; he said, "Bill, you cannot wear a nice watch in London anymore." I looked through my drawer, found the Bremont, and thought it was a cool watch. I put it on. I was staying in Mayfair and walked by the Bremont store the next day. I walked in, liked the watches, and bought eight of them for gifts. That night, I had dinner with the chair of Pershing Square Holdings, gave him a watch, and wrote a note. I asked who owned the company and they said two British brothers, the English brothers. I wrote them a note saying if you are interested in a partner, I would be interested, perhaps to help you grow. I have always liked watches—it is a father-son thing. I did a Zoom with them, and I ended up buying a minority stake. The thesis was a growth strategy, opening boutiques, but it didn't work out and they burned through capital. I view these as hobby investments. It was a Warren Buffett style investment—I didn't do due diligence, I just assessed the character of the people and shook hands. I helped recruit a new CEO, Davide Toronto. Things weren't working out between Davide and the board, and I sided with Davide. He improved the quality of the watches, but the old guard felt it was too much change too quickly. There was a board fight, and being an activist, I got involved, put in more capital, and bought effective control. I joined the board as non-executive chair, inserted my nephew to help fix things, and they have made amazing progress in the last year. It has been a fun thing. Other people buy sports teams; I own this little watch company and it is super cool. This is a watch that is going to the moon. I need to get one of those. Yeah, this is called the Supernova. The face has all these phosphorescent materials; it lights up at night and looks like solar arrays in space. It is a ceramic bezel, a Swiss movement—one of the highest quality—and phenomenal design. We have a lot of fans. I like wearing a watch where the guy doesn't know what I paid for it. Also, because they are less well-known, the thieves don't chop off your wrists for them yet. Talk to me about recruiting a new CEO. That is one of the things you have done as an activist. How do you find the best person in the world for that? In our experience, finding someone who has done it before is the lowest risk and highest return potential. Chipotle went through food safety issues and we needed someone; Brian Nicols's name kept coming up. We use expert networks to talk to people who used to work for him or compete against him. You can learn a lot by 360 views. One of my best skills—though it's not perfect—is that I have become a very good judge of people. You need someone super passionate, with high capability, energy, and character. You can assess that in an hour. I want to switch gears. What are people's biggest misconceptions about you? People say, "Bill, I had a different impression of you based on the media, but you seem like a really nice guy." Perhaps my Twitter expressions make people think I am a firebrand, but I am not a hostile person; I am just someone trying to get to the truth. The media positions people, and then people form misconceptions. The Herbalife thing did do some reputational damage because they called me out. Being a short seller, the populist thinks that is evil. Having that be in the rearview mirror—that was the end of 2012—is helpful. We haven't made an activist investment for more than a decade. Would you ever do it again? I don't think we have to be an activist in a traditional form. If we had a big stake and felt they were doing the wrong thing and it was worth the energy, we might. I don't know that we are ever going to run another proxy contest. I don't think you run a proxy contest against someone with 3 million Twitter followers; it ends badly for management. I think we have sufficient influence that we can get to the right answer with dialogue. Activism is when you are outside the boardroom and not invited in. Today, any company where we are a shareholder would give us board representation because we have a good reputation and we are long-term investors. We always end with the same question: what is success for you? My definition for Pershing Square is that I want investors to have a life-changing investment so that by the time they retire, they can do the things they want to do—pay for education, buy a house. For me, I celebrated my 60th birthday in May. I think about each year; I love the summer, and it goes by quickly. Most healthy people live into their 80s, so I have 20 to 25 years. I am hoping AI and doing a better job enables me to live meaningfully more than that. I had a grandmother who lived to almost 106. I want to have a significant life, and my definition has always been to have the greatest beneficent impact on the largest number of people. Success for me is having a significant life.

Bill Gates’s Blunt Warning on A.I. | The Ezra Klein Show

The Ezra Klein Show(575.0K)·73:50·2026-09-29
Bill GatesCo-founder, Microsoft Ezra KleinHost, The Ezra Klein Show
TL;DRBill Gates argues that current artificial intelligence advancements have crossed critical security thresholds regarding cyberattacks and bioterrorism, making voluntary industry self-regulation "insane" and demanding urgent government-mandated monitoring and global safeguards.

TL;DR

Bill Gates argues that current artificial intelligence advancements have crossed critical security thresholds regarding cyberattacks and bioterrorism, making voluntary industry self-regulation "insane" and demanding urgent government-mandated monitoring and global safeguards. Furthermore, he warns of an imminent white-collar and blue-collar job apocalypse driven by superhuman cognitive capabilities, requiring proactive societal interventions such as robot taxation and human-reserved job protections.

The AI Risk Reality: Crossing Dangerous Thresholds

  • Immediate Capabilities: Bill Gates states that AI models have crossed critical thresholds in early 2024 regarding cyberattacks and bioterrorism.
    • Cyber vulnerabilities: Next-generation models like *Mythos* can find security bugs and inject complex exploits into codebases that human engineers have reviewed safely for over 20 years.
    • Bio-threat democratization: Sophisticated molecules can now be engineered by small groups with malintent using the latest open-source AI tools, lowering the barrier for producing dangerous biological threats previously restricted to nation-states.
  • The Illusion of Self-Regulation: Bill Gates dismisses the argument made by leaders like Jensen Huang and Donald Trump that individual CEOs will simply refuse to release unsafe products.
    • Open-source models currently lack monitoring or filtering, allowing bad actors to remove safeguards entirely.
    • Relying purely on capitalism, liability lawsuits, and post-harm bankruptcy is insufficient for threats capable of causing tens or hundreds of millions of deaths.
  • The Need for a Supervisory Layer: Bill Gates stresses that society urgently needs a sovereign-enforced supervisory layer and mandatory platform monitoring to prevent catastrophic attacks before they happen.

Labor Disruption and the "Jobpocalypse"

  • Cognitive Scarcity Eliminated: Bill Gates argues that past technologies replaced physical labor while leaving human cognition as the scarce, valuable element. AI removes this final differentiator by working 24 hours a day, knowing every language, and operating at superhuman speeds.
  • Imminent White-Collar Displacement: While coding is the primary profession currently past the reliability threshold, accounting, legal work, telesales, and customer support will cross that threshold over the next few years.
  • Flawed Counter-Arguments: Bill Gates rejects the Jevons paradox and claims of a massive "relational sector" job boom, arguing that once an entire task is automated completely, the savings go into the token budget rather than human salaries.
  • Proposed Mitigations: To counter the transition, Bill Gates proposes two primary policy interventions:
    • Robot Taxation: Requiring companies that replace human workers with AI or robots to pay equivalent FICA or pension contributions to prevent social security funds from going bankrupt.
    • Human-Reserved Jobs: Designating specific societal sectors—such as childcare, elder care, and portions of medical care and education—as protected "nature reserves" for human employment.

Global Equity, Development, and the Gates Foundation

  • The Divergence in Impact: Bill Gates differentiates sharply between developed nations and low-income countries regarding AI's net utility.
    • Low-income countries: In places where people never meet a doctor or agricultural advisor, AI tools deployed on smartphones (such as helping a Yoruba-speaking woman in Nigeria or providing crop disease guidance to Indian farmers) represent an overwhelmingly positive force to be pushed forward as quickly as possible.
    • Rich countries: The displacement effects, job loss risks, and psychosocial challenges hit developed nations much faster and harder.
  • Foundation Focus: The Gates Foundation is actively accelerating AI utility for under-represented populations, announcing a five-year goal to enable 3.4 billion people speaking under-represented languages to use AI tools in their own language and voice.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Bill Gates predicts that white-collar professions including accounting, legal work, telesales, and telesupport will cross the reliability threshold and experience widespread automation over the next few years.
  • Bill Gates states that humanity faces a 20-year plus transition period during which society must manage the risks of cyber/bio events and mass job displacement.

Notable Quotes

"The folks who are in positions of authority are denying what is about to happen... You can't rely on the industry to self-regulate here. I mean it's just insane." — Bill Gates
"This is the most dangerous thing that humans have ever gone near. In other areas, do we just say, hey, release your drugs, there's no FDA... Do we just use the liability laws to try and keep humans safe? ...Say you kill 100 million, you want to use a lawsuit? I- I- I almost can't keep a straight face." — Bill Gates
"Once you replace human cognition as a scarce element, in fact, you blow it away in terms of working 24 hours a day, reading more, knowing every language, there is no scarcity that you're moving up to." — Bill Gates
Bill Gates is a fascinating person in the AI debate right now. He is somebody with experience in a number of the different perspectives that most people can only hold one of. He is a revolutionary technologist who built some of the foundations of the future that we're now living in. What can the computer do that a book can't? I mean, that's the foremost interactive process, isn't it? No, it's not. Not at all. An innovative word-processing program called Microsoft Word. This is absolutely revolutionary, of course. Then, of course, as C.E.O. of Microsoft, the world's largest software company. Some say it is the most successful company, the most dazzling new industry of the century. He has felt the momentum of corporate competition. And Microsoft, of course, is still in some of the race dynamics present in AI. We now have an incredible roster of seven new world-class models to keep everybody working at the absolute frontier. And then, as chair of the Gates Foundation, he's been working with governments around the world on regulatory issues, on poverty alleviation, on equity, for many, many years now. Very, very few people combine technological experience, corporate experience, and governmental experience in quite the way he does. Frankly, computers will be in any meaningful sense as smart as people at some point. So his recent essay on AI, where he says now that he is staking his reputation on trying to get people to see how bad what is coming might be—trying to get them to see that we are not ready for what is about to happen—was a real departure from what I've read from Gates previously to this. And then, when I sat and talked to him, how emphatic he is, how afraid he even seems to me to be that we are not ready for what we are building and that so many of the people in positions of authority are denying what is about to happen, was really striking. So, a really quite a call to arms. Bill Gates, welcome to the show. Great to see you. So, I wanted to begin with a clip we found of you on The David Letterman show from 1996, and I'm going to hand it over to you to play. Great. Is there something now beyond what we understand about computers that a—like 20 years ago we didn't fully understand computers, is there now another level of something, maybe we haven't even thought of it, maybe it's not even possible, maybe, you know, a whole different mechanism, a whole different software and hardware or is this going to be it now through the end of time? Mostly what we're working on now is the computer being a tool, a tool to help us learn or find other people with the same interests. Eventually, we may figure out how to make the computer think, but that turns out to be a very tough problem. In fact, there's been almost no progress made on it. So, nobody knows when that'll happen; some people think it'll never happen. Yeah, we don't want them to think, do we? Not really, I wouldn't think. It's a scary thought. So that was 30 years ago. Narrate for me how we went from that being a scary thought that might never happen to arguably the reality we're sitting here discussing today. Well, the notion that computation could provide thinking at a human level, you have Alan Turing talking about that before I'm born and even proposing a test of if you could be fooled in a conversation, that was called passing the Turing Test. And so the whole time I'm learning software, this idea of can we make software see or listen or read or write, you know, that's the holy grail. And when I do drop out of Harvard, I said to my co-founder Paul Allen, "Gosh, if there's a breakthrough in artificial intelligence while we're off selling basic interpreters and word processors, we'll feel bad." I might wish I would have stayed in academia. So that's 1975. Progress on AI is mostly going down dead ends like Prolog expert systems, and there's a small group, including Geoffrey Hinton and a few others, that are working on neural nets. And eventually, there's enough power actually coming from the graphics processor, that that idea, these highly statistical approaches start to show promise. And I was going down to see OpenAI on a regular basis to see the work they were doing and I challenged them, "Hey, if you can read a biology textbook and pass the advanced placement exam getting a perfect grade, which is a five, then you will prove that you are reading, that is encoding knowledge in an accessible form." And so it's six months before the public release that Sam, Greg, and Ilya come up and at my house, demonstrate to me getting a five on the AP exam, even on questions that I had made up that it couldn't possibly have seen, very complex biology problems, it was nearly perfect. And so that was shock number one. And then late last year when the Claude coding models had gotten super good and I can see that they are as good as I am, which is significantly my most developed talent because I was obsessed from age 13 to 24 that, could I write code as good or better than anyone? That's another moment where I go, "This is incredible." That the capability of doing long-running complex tasks has now gotten to the point that they are superhuman at writing code and in finding flaws in code. So what they're not superhuman at yet is deciding what to do, that kind of higher-level strategizing. Do you think that's far from being a capability for them? Well, definitely. If you're—our foundation does these strategy reviews, we spend two weeks in October to set how we're going to spend our 10 billion next year for 2027, and a year ago, somebody said, "Well, we should ask the AI what it thinks," and that was actually a pretty good joke back then because it wasn't coherent enough to see these things. This year, among the inputs to that discussion will be taking strategy notes and actually engaging in a dialogue with ChatGPT, Claude, Copilot, and even having them talk with each other. And in a few of the reviews, we'll actually have the AI sit in. In a few cases, we'll tell it, "Hey, only speak if we ask you," and then in a few other cases we'll say, "Hey, if you hear something you think is wrong or you hear us thinking, what are these statistics, please engage." So, we've gone from it being a joke to it will be a peer, not making any final decisions but it will be a peer in deep complex strategic discussions making a significant contribution. So in that Letterman interview, you said it would be a scary thought. Why then would you have said it would be a scary thought to have computers that think? Well, no one who's ever been fascinated or wanted to develop AI doesn't realize that it's incredibly scary that it will be better. Biological minds, it's amazing how general-purpose they are and that the optimization was staying alive, breeding, socializing with each other for survival and fertility, and yet we can write symphonies and play chess and even write some pretty cool software. And the idea when you move the template away from biology to silicon, you don't have these boundaries between individuals. You don't have a limited memory. The size of the brain is limited by the birth canal. It's why humans at first are very limited; we're very unusual in how helpless we are at birth because we've so optimized for having a large brain. But the silicon intelligence doesn't have these limitations. The idea of read every medical journal and see if there's anything that we didn't spot, that is what AIs do today. That's why, particularly for less common diseases, they are so superhuman at seeing a set of symptoms and being able to diagnose them. They can just keep more in their mind and see what things relate to each other that no human will ever be able to do. So if you don't retain control over it, you've evolved a species that will beat us, just in a very different realm. And so every AI company, whether it's DeepMind or OpenAI, they all say, "Okay, whatever goes on here, it can't just be driven by profit maximization. We have to have a charter that if we get to dangerous thresholds, we can exercise judgment that would be against profit maximization." Sadly, those mechanisms only work if there's only one company. So say OpenAI invented post-AGI and then they said, "No, we're going to bury this." If no one else ever did it, then fine, that Pandora's box stayed closed. But of course many companies work on this, and even OpenAI spawns Anthropic because they think that some of these safety issues aren't getting enough attention. So, no one involved with this takes lightly the idea of what world super-powerful AI creates. I want to hold on that race dynamic for a minute. Building the partner ecosystem with Sophos

OpenAI(2.1M)·13:44·10.8K views·2026-09-29
Claire LawlorDigital Native AD EMEA, OpenAI John PetersonCTO, Sophos
TL;DRThrough the Daybreak partnership, cybersecurity leader Sophos leverages OpenAI's frontier intelligence to automate threat investigations, slashing average response times from 38 minutes to 89 seconds.
Accelerating Frontier Intelligence: John Peterson (Sophos) explains that...

TL;DR

Through the Daybreak partnership, cybersecurity leader Sophos leverages OpenAI's frontier intelligence to automate threat investigations, slashing average response times from 38 minutes to 89 seconds.

The Defender's Window and Threat Landscape

  • Accelerating Frontier Intelligence: John Peterson (Sophos) explains that leading frontier models are developing sophisticated cyber capabilities that quickly filter down into open-weight models, driving faster exploitation paths and a massive increase in CVEs.
  • The Sophos Footprint: As a global cybersecurity company with 650,000 customers across every segment and sector, Sophos draws on four decades of experience to combat a huge variety of attacks.
  • Back-to-Basics Security: Peterson stresses that while technology is evolving rapidly, organizations must not get distracted; strong fundamentals—such as robust endpoint protection, multi-factor authentication (MFA) across all identity infrastructure, network segmentation, and solid patching programs—are more important than ever.

Partner Ecosystem Roles: Sophos vs. OpenAI

  • Sophos Domain Expertise: Contributes deep cybersecurity know-how, threat intelligence, incident response playbooks, and decades of operational experience to combat attacks at scale.
  • OpenAI Frontier Intelligence: Programs like Daybreak provide Sophos with cutting-edge frontier intelligence, allowing the company to scale its domain expertise and stay a step ahead of the attacker community to protect its 650,000 customers.

AI-Powered Threat Investigation with Sophos Fusion

  • Data Ingestion & Scale: The Sophos Fusion system—which powers Managed Detection and Response (MDR), EDR, and XDR products—gathers sensor data from over 500 third-party and proprietary integrations generating trillions of events daily.
  • Case Filtering: Trillions of daily events are distilled down into 1,000 to 2,000 cases per day, investigated across nine different security operations centers on behalf of customers.
  • Investigation Agents: Cases are fed into planning models that create a plan-execute-review loop, gathering customer context, detections, indicators of compromise, and threat landscape knowledge to conduct investigations and draft analyst summaries.
  • Efficiency & Talent Scaling: By leveraging Daybreak-powered agents, Sophos scales compute rather than personnel, helping alleviate the severe industry-wide shortage of cybersecurity talent and 24/7 security operations staff.

Customer Control and Human Judgment

  • Flexible Operating Modes: To match varying customer comfort levels and expertise, Sophos structures its service around three modes:
    • Notify: The SOC investigates fully but never takes independent response actions, only guiding and recommending to the customer.
    • Collaborate: The SOC conducts the full investigation and works directly with the customer, never acting without their direct involvement.
    • Authorize: Sophos is fully authorized to take response actions on behalf of the customer to keep them protected.
  • Human-in-the-Loop Safeguards: Agents handle a large volume of investigations, but human analysts remain essential for supervising actions, making trust-based decisions, and handling anything potentially destructive in a customer environment.

Notable Quotes

*"The leading frontier models are developing more and more sophisticated cyber capabilities... that's driving faster exploitation paths, more vulnerabilities, and the level of CVEs has gone up massively in the last few years."* — John Peterson
*"Historically, that was driven primarily by human expertise... with an average response time of around 38 minutes... Now, because of the agents that we've been able to build leveraging the Daybreak program, the average response time for cases... has reduced to about 89 seconds."* — John Peterson
*"Finding a threat, right, is is one thing, but deciding how to respond is really where trust and judgment matter."* — Claire Lawlor
I'm delighted to be joined by JP. What does the defender's window mean for Sophos and how do you protect your customers? Yeah, that's great. So, we think of the defender's window in terms of the pace of acceleration in frontier intelligence. The leading frontier models are developing more and more sophisticated cyber capabilities. Those are working their way down into the open-weight models as well, and that's driving faster exploitation paths and more vulnerabilities. We're seeing that in the CVEs as well; just the level of CVEs has gone up massively in the last few years. So, we think about it in terms of having an opportunity to take advantage of frontier intelligence through programs like Daybreak that allow us to stay a step ahead of the attackers. We look at that in terms of protecting ourselves at Sophos because, as a large cybersecurity company, we're obviously a target.
