Yeah, that's a good question. I think the way that I think about that is Sophos contains lots of cybersecurity domain experts. We've been in the cybersecurity business a long time, and it's our business to protect our customers. That domain expertise takes a variety of forms: threat intelligence, response playbooks when we're managing incidents on behalf of customers, and also just the expertise of our people. I look at it as Sophos bringing the domain expertise and the know-how to actually combat attacks at scale, but then programs like Daybreak and companies like OpenAI bring frontier intelligence that allows us to take that domain expertise and scale it to support all of our customers. That's what's been so powerful about the program and what it's allowed us to build. It's been fantastic to see that partnership go from strength to strength in recent months. Absolutely. I guess we're joined by some of the leading security leaders across EMEA here today. Maybe let's give them a little bit more of a practical example here. Can you walk us through an example of how a Daybreak-powered agent helps your team investigate a threat? Yep, absolutely. So, I think the place that I would start in explaining that is by just explaining the Sophos Fusion system, which is the system that powers the Managed Detection Response service at Sophos and all of our EDR and XDR products. These systems are built leveraging sensor data from over 500 different integrations with third-party security products, but also our own products as well. Those sensors are producing trillions of events every day. Those events get distilled down really into a small set of about 1,000 to 2,000 cases a day that nine different security operations centers are investigating on behalf of customers. Historically, that process of taking a case, conducting the investigation, and then remediating whatever the threat is, was driven primarily by human expertise. In the "before time," that human expertise and the processes we built around it allowed us to have around a 38-minute average investigation time on those cases, which, 18 months ago, was better than 96% of professional security operations centers. But now, because of the agents that we've been able to build leveraging the Daybreak program, the average response time for cases that are leveraging the agents we've built has reduced to about 89 seconds. About half of the cases that we handle are now being automated by agents that we've developed using the Daybreak models. That's been transformative in terms of the effort that it takes for us to actually conduct those investigations, but it also results in better customer outcomes. To provide a little more detail about how that actually works in practice, we have these cases that come into the security operations team, and the cases themselves are now handled primarily by an investigation agent. We've developed a system that takes all of the information from the case—customer context, detections, indicators of compromise, and knowledge of the threat landscape—and it feeds that into a planning model. That then creates a plan-execute-review loop; it creates an investigation plan, conducts the investigation steps, and then creates a summary for the analysts to review and a set of recommended response actions. We have agents that we've built as well that handle some of the response. It's been transformative because it allows us to respond more quickly, but it also gives customers broadly a more consistent outcome, which is what we're really in the business of doing. I guess given that you're halving that time to response, where are those efforts then being placed? Where are you putting the additional time and effort, and how are you as a leader making those decisions? Yeah, a big part of the value is—well, let me take a step back. Cyber security expertise has always been in short supply. One of the reasons that our MDR business has been so successful, and why other MDR businesses have also been successful, is because it's actually hard to find security operations analysts that have the expertise to protect customers. It's hard to staff your own SOC and have 24/7 response capabilities. This has allowed us to help with that talent, and to use the talent that we have in the organization more effectively and efficiently so that we can scale the compute as opposed to the personnel supporting the MDR business. Yeah, I think that's great to hear, JP. I think there's not a person in this room that probably doesn't feel that skills gap. Finding a threat is one thing, but deciding how to respond is really where trust and judgment matter. So, how do you decide when to act on what an agent does find? That's a great question. At Sophos, the way that we've structured our MDR business from the beginning takes this problem into account, because it's a problem if it's humans doing it, and it's a problem if it's agents doing it. We have three basic operating modes. First is called "notify," and in that mode, our SOC will investigate a case fully on behalf of customers, but we'll never take a response independently; we guide the customer and we recommend response, but we never take that on their behalf. Second is "collaborate," and in that mode, we conduct the full investigation, but then we work with the customer and never take a response without their direct involvement. The third is "authorize," and in that mode, we are fully authorized to take response actions on behalf of customers and keep them protected. We're comfortable operating with customers in any of those different modes. We structured it that way so that we could meet the customer where they are and align our service with their level of expertise and comfort. All of those same principles apply to agents as well as to humans. We want to ensure that we're responding quickly and using our analysts as efficiently as we can, but we also recognize the need to ensure that human judgment is applied whenever there's potentially a destructive response that an agent could take. Anything that we don't feel comfortable with an agent handling, we will pass off for human judgment. So, there's still a lot of human judgment in the response process, even though the agents are automating a lot of the investigations. And how do you see that evolving over time, JP? Excuse me, I'm a little bit tired after last night. I think you're doing great. Yeah, the way that we see it evolving is that the response capabilities are going to continue to become more sophisticated, and we're going to continue to broaden the series of use cases that we are addressing with the agents we've built. But we're always going to have humans—at least at the moment, I feel like we're always going to have humans—supervising anything that could be potentially destructive in a customer environment. Because at the end of the day, our mission is keeping customers protected and operational, but also giving them the control to decide whether that's full authorization or notification only. Exactly. Okay, great. I suppose to close, let's bring it back to the security leaders that we have here in the room. What would the first step to getting started with Sophos really look like? I think the way that I would explain that is just that in this environment where the defender window is closing, frontier intelligence is accelerating, and the capability of relatively novice attackers to make a real impact is high, doing the fundamentals well is more important than it's ever been. That means having patching programs to deal with the vulnerabilities and the CVEs that are being released by many different vendors and by the open-source community, but also making sure that you have good fundamentals in place across your infrastructure. Solid endpoint protection is more important than it's ever been. Additionally, having basic things like MFA in place across all of your identity infrastructure, having strong network segmentation, and then having a strong security operations program that wraps all of that is more important than it's ever been. Sophos plays in all those different places, and we would be happy to talk to anybody that's interested in learning more. So really, you're not saying to throw out the fundamentals here and forget all those basics. I am not saying that, no. It's not controversial what I'm saying, I don't think, but I think we get distracted by the pace that the technology is moving. Of course, we want to do everything we can to leverage agents to help security operations teams deal with the increased workload, but fundamentals like endpoint protection are just more important today than they ever have been. Thanks a million, JP. It's been great chatting, and I'm sure there's many people in the audience who'd love to pick your brain this afternoon. So JP is around; he will be here for the remainder of the afternoon and available if you want to buy him a drink at our open bar shortly. Thanks a million for your time, JP. I'm going to be leaving the stage shortly, but next up I'm delighted to introduce Tom Duff Gordon, who is OpenAI's policy VP for EMEA. Thank you.

Factory CEO on why AGI is already here

Sources Podcast(70.5K)·50:16·8.1K views·2026-09-29
Matan GrinbergCEO, Factory Alex HeathHost, Sources Podcast
TL;DRMatan Grinberg, CEO of Factory, argues that AGI is already here and that the future of software development will be model-agnostic, enterprise-focused, and IDE-free.
Transition from IDE Autocomplete: Factory was founded on April 17, 2023, at a time when the market was fixated...

TL;DR

Matan Grinberg, CEO of Factory, argues that AGI is already here and that the future of software development will be model-agnostic, enterprise-focused, and IDE-free.

Evolution of AI Coding & The Shift to Autonomous Agents

  • Transition from IDE Autocomplete: Factory was founded on April 17, 2023, at a time when the market was fixated on GitHub Copilot and IDE autocompletion.
  • The "IDE-Free" Bet: Factory's early website slogan was "The future is IDE free," which caused many founding engineer candidates to drop out before the thesis became industry consensus.
  • Rapid Adoption Curve: While the SF AI startup community adopted agents early, broader enterprise behavior change took off at the turn of 2025 into 2026, spurred by figures like Andre Karpathy sharing how they used coding agents.
  • Token Usage Explosion: Enterprise token usage is now 10xing year-over-year, and the daily behavior of engineers has changed more in the past three years than in the 15 years prior.

Factory’s Unique Enterprise Focus and Model-Agnostic Philosophy

  • Position in the Stack: Factory positions itself as model-agnostic and empowers developers directly, whereas model providers (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, SpaceX, Cursor) build their own closed applications, and companies like Palantir or Accenture deploy forward-deployed engineers (FDEs) to do the work for clients.
  • Enterprise Focus: While Factory offers a self-serve product, it laser-focuses on the enterprise because that is where tedious, low-leverage, and messy work lives (e.g., legacy migrations and decades-old codebases).
  • The Software Factory and Droid: Factory's core product is not a single agent, but a "software factory" that makes software self-improving. Their primary coding agent is named Droid.
  • Model Routing: Factory integrates deeply with Cursor, GitHub Copilot, Claude Code, and Codex, running a built-in model router that dynamically allocates problems based on performance, latency, and token cost to prevent enterprise lock-in.

Scaling Up: Funding, Team Growth, and Acquisitions

  • Team Expansion: Factory started 2023 with 30 people (two in sales), is scaling to 300 people by the end of the year, and has opened international offices in London, Sydney, and Tokyo to handle massive enterprise demand.
  • Acquisition Strategy: Factory frequently acquires early-stage companies not because they failed, but to bring in high-IQ, high-agency teams whose ambition scales faster under Factory with a go-to-market sales army behind them.
  • Margin Profile: Grinberg states that Factory has built a margin-positive AI business in 2026 by focusing on enterprise value rather than reselling raw tokens at a negative margin.

The Post-AGI World, Safety, and Liability

  • AGI is Already Here: Grinberg asserts that we are currently living in a post-AGI world because the capabilities of today's tools would have seemed unfathomably superintelligent just a few years ago.
  • The Lava Pit Analogy on Liability: Criticizing current AI safety discourse, Grinberg and host Alex Heath compare AI models escaping sandboxes (like the Hugging Face incident) to inviting a guest into an apartment with a pit of lava at the door—the lab is strictly at fault and legally liable, not the technology itself.
  • Regulatory Stance: Grinberg praised a recent policy shift by Dr. Michael Katzios requiring oversight for closed models while exempting open models, calling it a genius stroke to support the US open-source ecosystem.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Model-Agnostic Imperative: Grinberg predicts that enterprises will refuse to standardize on a single closed provider, making model independence a permanent requirement for robust business infrastructure.
  • AI-Generated Code in Production: Grinberg predicts that within the next couple of years, certain types of code and enterprise use cases will have to be entirely AI-generated because they will be more robust than human-written code.
  • Redefining Engineering: Grinberg predicts that while there will be vastly more people doing work currently called "engineering," they may not identify as traditional software engineers, likening it to how smartphones turned everyone into photographers without making them professional photographers.

Notable Quotes

"If you gave proper context and properly subdivided the problem, you could do it pretty consistently. And so then making autonomous agents was just a matter of providing the right context and properly subdividing the problem." — Matan Grinberg
"We are living in a post-AGI world right now... These tools are insane. Like the things that—if I showed you what you could do in Factory four years ago, you would freak out." — Matan Grinberg
"If I invite you over to my apartment and I have a pit of lava right past my front door... I'm not going to then go say, 'Hey guys, we need to check everyone's houses to see if they have pits of lava at the front door.' It's like, no, I need to go to jail for inviting you in and having you fall into this pit." — Matan Grinberg

There's an ambiguous problem and what they do is they define here are the constraints like the multi-dimensional constraints that we need to satisfy in order to solve this problem correctly and then you have to go and write the code that satisfies those constraints. now you don't actually have to do the writing of the code, but still the source of the alpha is those constraints. So like to to you know I think it's a very pathy way that you put it which is like the code itself is cheap. The thing that's not cheap is like what are the constraints that the code needs to satisfy? Like if we have a very multi-dimensional problem and there's like it's like a constrained optimization. the optimal solution, the optimal setup of what you need to satisfy, that is very difficult to figure out. Once you figure that out, writing the code that satisfies those things is not as hard. And now it's getting even easier with tools like this. But figuring out the nuanced solution, that is why engineers get paid so much money is because they're the best systems thinkers in the world to think about and figure out what is that optimal solution. Let's talk about this model router you're building. I'm very obsessed with this idea of model routing and systems that abstract away which model to do in which case or task. And this is core to what factory does. And it sounds like strategically it's also what maybe would give you more room to grow an enterprise because enterprises want optionality. They don't want to be locked into a closed frontier model as as best as the closed frontier models are trying to do that. I'd be curious to hear where that idea originated. Was that always part of the founding of factory? Was you were going to be a model layer and you were going to route people around or was that something you saw more recently? Explain that a little more. >> Yeah, this is this has been a bet from the very beginning which is basically like the world we want to live in is one where there is not one model provider that is better than all the others. And importantly, there's actually a world I think most people want because that gives them kind of ultimate monopolistic tendencies if there's one model that is, you know, supreme over all others. and then as we serve enterprises, we want to make sure that they get the best goods and services for the lowest cost. And the way that you do that is by having a router that can dynamically change based on performance, based on latency, based on cost. It allows you to do like just you know free market like resource allocation. Here is a problem. Here is the type of kind of intelligence that we want to allocate to it. so we've been model agnostic from the start and I think it's really important for every enterprise to have this model agnostic stance because I mean who knows one day there might be one model that's great the next month as we've been seeing another one shoots way above and you want to make sure you're not locked in to the one that's now no longer the frontier. but you can as the models come out be dynamically adjusting that performance and also there going to be certain tasks where maybe it doesn't need the frontier of intelligence and you want to actually do something very cheap but very fast or there might be certain problems where you're actually like I don't care how long this takes just get it done by the end of the week and you want to get something cheaper because it can take longer and just being flexible to all these different options is something that we you know that's part of why we provide this service to enterprises and it's also There's just so much fatigue of like if you're an individual engineer, you cannot keep track of all of the models that come out and which one is now on the purto frontier of which cost and performance and all that and like to some degree if you're in a rush in the morning and like you want some toast. You don't give a where the electricity in your toaster came from. It's like you just want some good toast. You want to eat your toast and get get on with your day. and that is kind of the experience that we want people to have with the router that we have built into our into our agent, which is like if you're in a rush, you shouldn't have to care what's actually happening under the hood. You just want to get the task done and do it. And then there might be some cases where like, you know, here's a problem where I want this model in particular and give them the ability to manually select that. But then otherwise, like if you're in a rush, do the router and know that it's going to do a good job. >> So it sounds like the bare case for factory is that one model kind of takes it all and wins. >> Correct. and and you're the company is literally betting its future on the fact that that will not be the case. Former Google CEO Eric Schmidt: The Road to Superintelligence

Blackstone(75.1K)·33:00·2026-09-29
Eric SchmidtCEO, Relativity Space Christine AndersonGlobal Head of Corporate Affairs, Blackstone
TL;DREric Schmidt and Blackstone executives discuss the massive CapEx buildout in AI, power, and data centers, noting that AI is shifting from infrastructure to real-world usage like Meta’s Muse and Anthropic’s scaling portfolio adoption.
Schmidt argues that age brings a license for...

TL;DR

Eric Schmidt and Blackstone executives discuss the massive CapEx buildout in AI, power, and data centers, noting that AI is shifting from infrastructure to real-world usage like Meta’s Muse and Anthropic’s scaling portfolio adoption. Schmidt argues that age brings a license for greater risk, predicts long-reasoning AI will transform business and science, and stresses that young people should study deep reasoning above all else.

Private Credit and Broad Economic Growth

  • Headline Performance: Private credit headlines have quieted down significantly because actual portfolio performance has far outperformed doom predictions made earlier in the year, according to Gilles Dellaert.
  • Broad Economic Playbook: Lenders are active across global markets beyond AI—engaging with industrial, aviation, and telecom companies to finance growth, strategic M&A, and CapEx backed by real assets with inflation protection.

AI Infrastructure and Market Momentum

  • Power and Compute Constraints: Gilles Dellaert notes that power and compute are the most constrained segments of the global AI supply chain, creating supply-demand imbalances that drive private financing opportunities.
  • Market Adoption Milestones: Winfield Sickles reports that Meta shares jumped roughly 13% after launching its new AI agent, *Muse*, which has already accumulated millions of downloads, signaling a transition from infrastructure build-out to real-world usage.
  • Portfolio Spend Surge: Anthropic spend across Blackstone portfolio companies, borrowers, and GP stakes PortCos grew approximately 27 times over the past 12 months.
  • Chip Maker Benchmarks: AMD recently became the fourth U.S. chip maker to surpass a $1 trillion market cap.
  • Economic Indicators: Winfield Sickles highlights that the Fed delivered its first rate hike since 2023 with a unanimous vote, the 5-year U.S. Treasury yield moved above 5% for the first time in nearly 20 years, and global government bonds reached an average yield of 4% (a post-global financial crisis high).

Technology Evolution, Scale, and Superintelligence

  • Eric Schmidt’s Tech Tenure: Eric Schmidt reflects on 55 years in technology, starting with mainframes and noting that as people get older, they should take *more* risk because they have less to lose in service of democracy and freedom.
  • Scale-Free Learning: Schmidt explains that modern AI gains occur in scale-free environments where systems undergo recursive self-improvement loops, getting smarter on their own without needing external data.
  • Recent Mathematical Breakthroughs: Computers recently solved 10 major math problems in the last month, including Navier-Stokes equations governing fluid flows, which improve algorithmic answers for aviation, gas efficiency, and rocket travel.
  • Long Reasoning Capability: Schmidt identifies the arrival of long reasoning (chains of 1,000 to 2,000 steps or up to 8 hours of reasoning) as the most important recent advancement, allowing AIs to function as 24-hour agent thought partners.
  • AI-Native CEOs and Workflows: Eric Schmidt advises CEOs to operate as "AI-native CEOs" by automating execution, connecting factory floor computers (as he does at Relativity Space), and combining public open-source data with proprietary corporate data for data fusion and business insights.

Data Centers and the Economics of AI Factories

  • Revenue Determination: Eric Schmidt points out that AI company revenues are completely determined by their data centers, shifting software economics by requiring heavy hardware investments.
  • Electricity Demand: AI data center demand is projected to reach 11% of U.S. electricity demand by 2030, up historically from 2% to 3%.
  • Megawatt Economics: Jaz Khaira explains that companies can generate $50 to $70 million in revenue per megawatt against all-in costs of $12 to 15 million (including chips and depreciating cycles), driving firms to plow today's cash into purchasing tomorrow's AI factories.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Fusion Energy Timeline: Eric Schmidt predicts that fusion energy will likely be achieved within 2 to 3 years, with commercial realization in 3 to 5 years.
  • Superintelligence Consensus Timeline: Schmidt notes the "San Francisco consensus" among developers expects superintelligence leaps in 2 years, though he personally believes it will take at least a decade or happen within his lifetime.

Notable Quotes

"As you get older, you should take on more risk, because you have less to lose, right? And so you might as well try the things that you thought were impossible." — Eric Schmidt
"The biggest and most important thing that has occurred in the last year is the arrival of long reasoning, deep reasoning. And when I say long reasoning, I mean 8 hours of reasoning." — Eric Schmidt
"If you're a non-technical person, you should figure out how to use these tools to make your dreams and your realities extraordinarily scaled... If you're a technical person, use the same tools to invent stuff and to invent stuff that changes the world." — Eric Schmidt

Gary Gensler Talks AI Competition with China | Bloomberg Talks

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·13:29·791 views·2026-09-29
Gary GenslerFormer Chair, SEC Tom KeeneHost, Bloomberg Podcasts Paul SweeneyHost, Bloomberg Podcasts
TL;DRFormer SEC Chairman Gary Gensler warns that the current AI investment boom is twice the scale of the 1990s internet bubble at 2.5% of the U.S.
economy, posing serious revenue challenges for domestic market leaders like OpenAI and Anthropic due to cheaper Chinese models and...

TL;DR

Former SEC Chairman Gary Gensler warns that the current AI investment boom is twice the scale of the 1990s internet bubble at 2.5% of the U.S. economy, posing serious revenue challenges for domestic market leaders like OpenAI and Anthropic due to cheaper Chinese models and private credit exposure.

AI Capital Boom & Competitive Threats

  • Investment Scale: Massive Capital Inflow: The AI investment boom—encompassing data centers and related infrastructure—now accounts for 2.5% of the U.S. economy, roughly twice the relative size of the internet boom during the Alan Greenspan era (which peaked at 1.0% to 1.5%).
  • The Chinese Model Challenge: Lower-Cost Alternatives: Chinese AI models, while potentially two to six months behind at the frontier, are being developed with significantly lower spending.
    • US companies are increasingly utilizing Chinese open-weight models for reasoning tasks, bypassing high-cost domestic options from OpenAI and Anthropic.
    • This threatens the revenue propositions necessary to justify heavy U.S. capital expenditures.

Alignment, Security, and Guardrails

  • Autonomous Agent Risks: Vulnerability Discovery: AI models are demonstrating advanced coding and autonomous replication capabilities.
    • Gensler highlighted a recent Open AI summer incident where 1,200 simultaneous AI agents were deployed to search for vulnerabilities, successfully finding them at Hugging Face.
  • Alignment & Deception: Models increasingly show reasoning capabilities that involve deception and escaping guarded sandboxes.
  • Regulatory Challenges: Ex-Ante Guardrails Missing: Neither the current administration nor Congress has effectively instituted ex-ante guardrails, though Congress is beginning to lean into the issue.
    • Furthermore, open-weight models create a loophole where threat actors can remove built-in safety guardrails (akin to illegally removing a catalytic converter from an automobile).

Financial System & Retail Exposure

  • Private Credit Vulnerability: Retailization Risks: High-net-worth and retail investors are being sold illiquid private credit products tied to AI data center debt under the false promise of liquidity.
    • When the AI trade eventually retrenches and debt paper drops below par value (e.g., to 80 or 90 cents on the dollar), retail investors seeking redemptions will face severe liquidity traps.
  • Banking & Systemic Risks: Deposit Flight and Herding:
    • AI-driven tools (such as Facebook Muse) could help consumers find higher-yielding deposits and money market funds, accelerating bank deposit flight (though likely slower than Torsten Sløk's notes suggest).
    • If financial institutions broadly adopt similar AI models, it could lead to dangerous market "herding" and systemic risk.

Geopolitics & Political Economy

  • Democratic Party & Capitalism: Responsible Capitalism: Gensler argues that both major U.S. parties are experiencing transitions driven by a changing economy and a U.S. step-back from global leadership.
    • The Democratic Party maintains a deep belief in capitalism, but favors "responsible capitalism" supported by regulatory guardrails akin to the Franklin Roosevelt-era protections established 90 years ago.

Notable Quotes

*"The AI trade that our capital markets is hovering on top of... is an investment boom that's about twice what the internet was. Back to Alan Greenspan, he was watching as a lot of tech companies were investing and it added up to about one to one and a half percent of the economy... This is now 2.5% of our economy."* — Gary Gensler
*"The Chinese are competing with the US. The US wants to stay ahead, but the Chinese are doing something called openweight models. This is a competitive challenge because US companies can use it and they can use it far cheaper and it undermines the revenue proposition for Anthropic and Open AI."* — Gary Gensler
*"When, and it's not a question of if, but when we have a retrenchment in the big AI trade, there's a lot of debt on those data centers that is in this complex in the private credit complex. And so... that's when the retail public's going to be saying, 'Wait, this paper's no longer worth a hundred cents on the dollar.'"* — Gary Gensler
Bloomberg Audio Studios, podcasts, radio, news. Joining us now, someone that should have been there yesterday, Gary Gensler, of course, a legendary in Wall Street and the former chairman of the Securities and Exchange Commission. Gary Gensler, what did Alan Greenspan do within the ups and the downs, the right and the wrong, how did he advance the American experiment? Well, Alan was a real public servant and I had the honor to work with him. I couldn't be there this weekend just because of things up here in Boston where I'm at right now for MIT, but he really understood the markets and deeply understood the markets. And I think in the 1990s he sorted through this new technology, the internet, and what it was doing to our economy. And I think a little bit before others, he understood how to control inflation but still leave enough room for the—I don't know if you want to call them the animal spirits—but that investment boom in the 1990s. I look, Gary, then let me jump forward right away to what you are working on at MIT with your wonderful podcast as well. The new internet is the AI, the news flow. This morning, Paul and I opened the show. Gary, I've never seen a company do a red herring like the Reuters report on Anthropic. I mean, to be polite, Gary, it's bizarre. Are these companies legitimate? Well, they have a remarkable thing how they've pulled in so many users so rapidly, Anthropic, OpenAI, and the like. But the AI trade that our capital markets is hovering on top of is an investment boom that's about twice what the internet was. So back to Alan Greenspan, he was watching as a lot of tech companies were investing and it added up to about 1 to 1.5% of the economy. It was considered at the end a bubble. This is now 2.5% of our economy. I'm talking about all the data centers and so forth. And here's the challenge for Anthropic and OpenAI: the Chinese models. They might not be quite at the frontier, but if they're two to six months behind us, regardless of how they do it—whether they use what's called distillation or not—and they're spending a lot less in the Chinese economy, a lot of US companies are saying, "Look, I'll use the Chinese model to do my reasoning and maybe I won't use OpenAI or Anthropic." So that's the challenge for them. They're spending so much, but will it lead to the revenues to justify that spending? Gary, so much capital, as you well know, has been raised in equity markets, fixed income markets to support the growth of AI. Even some of the founding voices of these companies at OpenAI and Anthropic, they are calling for some guardrails on this technology. How do you think about that? Well, I think that they're real challenges. AI's capabilities, the ability to do coding on its own—you know, write the software—the ability to replicate itself and then send agents out on the internet. OpenAI released that report about an incident this summer where the agents multiplied to 1,200 different agents simultaneously looking for vulnerabilities, and they found them at Hugging Face. And the last thing I'd say is beyond their capabilities, alignment: are the models aligned with their human overlords, if you wish, we the humans? And more and more they're showing that they have some reasoning on how they deceive and how they jump out of guarded sandboxes. So, this is a real challenge. I know the president calls it a hoax. I differ with the president on that. Gensler with us folks, the former chairman, I should say, of the Securities and Exchange Commission. His good work for years at Goldman Sachs noted as well. I got to get to some other matters here. It's amazing. Gensler comes on, and the Bloomberg reporters in Washington are brutal. You should see questions. I mean, they want to know what he thinks about Yankees/Red Sox. I mean, it's too much there. One more AI question, Gary. I got to go here. We've had other AIs. We didn't have guardrails on the trains. We didn't have guardrails on the miracle of pharmaceuticals and antibiotics, tetracycline, and all that. And God, who in God's name is going to institute the guardrails? I don't see any institution with motivations to on an ex-ante basis get out front on AI technology. Where is it? Well, currently it's not in this administration, nor in the Congress, though I would say the Congress is starting to really lean into it. But look, Tom, we put guardrails on our capital markets so that those capital markets work better. And they're not perfect, but when the Securities and Exchange Commission was created, it was about investor protection. It was about making sure that the markets worked better, created the public good, that you could trust the markets, right? So, I would say on AI, it's possible. But here's the wrinkle, and it's a big, consequential wrinkle. The Chinese are competing with the US. The US wants to stay ahead, but the Chinese are doing something called open-weight models. This is a competitive challenge because US companies can use it and they can use it far cheaper, and it undermines the revenue proposition for Anthropic and OpenAI. It also undermines—can you put guardrails on something that then the user, the threat actor, can maybe take off? Think about those people that sometimes say, "I'll take my catalytic converter off my automobile." And it's not legal, but a threat actor could say, "Yeah, I'm just going to take the guardrails off." Gary, I got a whole group of reporters that do this 24/7. The pit bull terrier down in Washington, Nola White. Oh, boy. She's a threat to society. She covers enforcement for us, folks. And she's got a brilliant note here to me, Gary, about the experiment of mom-and-pop retail access to private credit and to all this other stuff that's illiquid as well. How is that experiment going? And she wants to know, Nola wants to know, does it keep you up at night that we're retailizing—that's my word, okay—I just want to impress Gensler that we're retailizing retail into things they shouldn't be going into? Tom, you impress me every time you go out to that Jackson Hall conference and you have such access to everybody that's in that meeting. So don't worry about it. Look, I do think that there's a mismatch that the retail public, even high-net-worth retail public, is being sold private credit. And then they're also being told they can get some liquidity, and you really can't. If you want to get the higher returns, you need to put your risk capital at work for longer periods of time and not have those redemptions. Now, look, it's working so far, but I would say when—and it's not a question of if, but when we have a retrenchment in the big AI trade—there's a lot of debt on those data centers that is in this complex, in the private credit complex. And so, to your colleague's question, that's when the retail public's going to be saying, "Wait, this paper's no longer worth a hundred cents on the dollar, it's worth 80 or 90 cents or even lower. I'd like to get out." And what happens then? That's a real challenge. So Gary, Torsten Slok, economist at Apollo, was out with a note recently raising some questions about how the rise of AI agents might affect the financial system. How do you think about that? We had the CEO of LPL Financial in yesterday and he was voicing some of those concerns as well. How do you think about AI in the context of just consumers and financial services? Well, I'm in the classroom teaching artificial intelligence and money, which is addressing those very questions with some terrific MIT students, and Simon and I of course talk about it repeatedly on our Power and Consequences podcast. My thought is three things. One is for consumers, they might actually get some better automated access to higher-rate deposits. That's what Apollo's Mr. Slok was pointing out. He was saying investors might be able to find better than the very low rates that most bank deposits get. So that's a net plus. But two, if you're using Facebook Muse and finding those higher-rate deposits, maybe moving into money market funds as well, then the banks might have deposit flight. I think it will happen slower than his note suggested, but it's real. The other risk is if all of finance starts using the similar models, then you might have herding. And I wrote a paper on this a while back; it could lead to real systemic risk. And then thirdly, it's just the overall—when we have a retrenchment, and at some point in time this boom will plateau and even turn the other way—when that occurs, then as Alan Greenspan would have liked to hear his friend Warren Buffett say, when the tide goes out, you see who's swimming with their trunks off. Gary, I mean, it just feels like—and I'm sure you see it every day in your classroom—the young folks are just embracing AI for its own sake, it's just become a part of their life as opposed to embracing it like Tom and I are trying to do. At some point, that's going to, I would think, impact how they invest, how they save, how they allocate their own capital. Is that a good thing from your perspective? Absolutely. Look, I'm kind of bullish and optimistic that we humans will figure it out, but there's real risk. We've talked about it on this program. There are existential risks about AI agents misaligned or not aligned with their human users, right? That threat actors will use it and it will disrupt markets. And so we have to build cybersecurity and cyber resilience way better. But I also think it will transform financial markets and that's a period of time of uncertainty. We've seen this in prior booms. You mentioned railroads and pharmaceuticals, and we've talked about the internet. It's all there. It all has some uncertainty, and then you have the geopolitical competition with China, and China's not taking their foot off the gas pedal. Gary, I got to be a smart alec at MIT in the back of the room with my hand up asking Professor Gensler a question. Gensler, the Clinton administration, the Obama administration, CFO for Hillary Clinton's campaign, working for President Biden, etc. From where you sit now, is there a tinge to the Democratic party experiment of being anti-capitalist right now? And so, we've talked about artificial intelligence, but also the US is stepping back from its leadership on the world stage. And we're in a—I would say a war in Iran that doesn't have any clear resolution and has been very damaging to our economy and to our role in the world. I think that's what the Democratic party is mostly looking at. But I would say that there's a deep belief in capitalism, but it's got to be responsible capitalism. And just as we put guardrails on our capital markets to make them work better, that Franklin Roosevelt did that some 90 years ago with Congress, I think we'll find our way to do that on AI, but right now there's real risks. Gary Gensler, thank you so much. Greatly appreciated with Simon Johnson at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Thank you.

JPMorgan, Ford to Invest Billions in Michigan Manufacturing

Bloomberg Tech(739.0K)·15:19·7.4K views·2026-09-29
Jamie DimonCEO, JPMorgan Chase Jim FarleyCEO, Ford Gretchen WhitmerGovernor, Michigan
TL;DRJPMorgan Chase, Ford Motor Company, and Michigan Governor Gretchen Whitmer launched "Michigan Lift," an industrial innovation program backed by a $1 billion purchasing commitment from Ford to onshore advanced manufacturing and supply chains.
Program Overview: Launched in...

TL;DR

JPMorgan Chase, Ford Motor Company, and Michigan Governor Gretchen Whitmer launched "Michigan Lift," an industrial innovation program backed by a $1 billion purchasing commitment from Ford to onshore advanced manufacturing and supply chains.

Michigan Lift Initiative & Industrial Onshoring

  • Program Overview: Launched in Detroit at Michigan Central, "Michigan Lift" aims to fix vulnerabilities caused by past offshoring by connecting local innovators with capital and customers.
  • Ford's Commitment: Ford Motor Co. CEO Jim Farley committed $1 billion in purchasing for new companies building advanced manufacturing capabilities, rare earth magnets, and aluminum supply chain components.
  • Cross-Sector Collaboration: JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon emphasized that rebuilding domestic manufacturing requires cooperation between business, state government, and financial institutions to provide reliable orders and production scale.
  • Geographic Scope: While primarily a Michigan-focused initiative leveraging the state's engineering talent and industrial DNA, leaders noted that trusted allies like Canadian suppliers heavily integrated into the auto industry can participate.

U.S. Economic Preeminence & Global Trade

  • National Security & Economy: Jamie Dimon argued in a *Wall Street Journal* op-ed that a dominant military is predicated on having the world's best economy and the U.S. dollar as the reserve currency.
  • Alliances & Trade: Dimon advocated for strengthening trade relationships with Western allies (Europe, UK, Japan, Australia, Mexico, Canada, South Korea), noting they comprise 60% of global GDP and share 40 military alliances and 20 free trade agreements.
  • Capital Market Advantages: Dimon contrasted the vibrant, multi-layered U.S. capital markets (venture capital, private equity, debt, and preferred markets) with stagnant European markets that lack comparable risk-taking capital and R&D funding.

Skilled Trades Deficit & Education

  • The Skilled Labor Crisis: Jim Farley stated the U.S. faces a crisis with 1.7 million open skilled trade jobs, noting that high school and college advisers overwhelmingly recommend four-year degrees while only 2% recommend trade schools.
  • Ford's Fact-Finding Report: Ford is publishing its first report on the facts surrounding the U.S. skilled trade deficit to raise awareness and help solve national supply chain resilience.
  • State Workforce Efforts: Governor Gretchen Whitmer highlighted Michigan's investments in upskilling, including free community college, accessible apprenticeships, and a workforce ranked number four in the country by *Site Selector* magazine, supported by the highest per-capita concentration of engineers in the nation.

Macroeconomic Pressures & Capital Allocation

  • Inflation and Interest Rates: Jamie Dimon acknowledged that higher interest costs and rising bond yields hit small and medium-sized businesses disproportionately, but argued that long-term national safety and getting inflation down transcend short-term market fluctuations.
  • Crowding Out Capital: Addressing capital availability amid massive AI investments, Dimon stated that capital crowding out is primarily driven by governments around the world borrowing record, inflationary sums of money rather than private productive investments.
  • Security & Resiliency Initiative: Dimon noted that JPMorgan's broader security and resiliency initiative (approaching its one-year anniversary of a targeted $1.5 trillion goal) has already invested $5 billion directly into pre-public companies in sectors like drones, nuclear power, rare earths, and semiconductors.

Automotive Competitiveness & Innovation

  • Global Speed and Competition: Jim Farley addressed the intense global competition from Chinese automakers moving at "light speed," stating that Ford developed a small U.S. skunkworks team in California and Michigan to out-innovate rivals.
  • New EV Development: Ford is engineering a new $30,000 electric vehicle—built in Louisville, Kentucky, and engineered in California and Michigan—designed to be fully competitive with global low-cost producers like BYD.

Notable Quotes

"We're trying to fix a problem onshoring things that that were off shored in the name of pennies on the dollar to make here in The US where we needed to be independent, even defend ourselves." — Jim Farley
"If you wanna be safe, have the best military in the world. It's predicated on having the best economy in the world, and that is what makes the dollar reserve currency." — Jamie Dimon
"We know how to build things. Michigan, that's who we are in our DNA. We were, you know, the arsenal of democracy. This is about homeland security. It's about advanced manufacturing." — Gretchen Whitmer

JPMorgan's Dimon, Gov. Whitmer and Ford's Farley Talk the Michigan Lift | Bloomberg Talks

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·15:54·250 views·2026-09-29
Jamie DimonCEO, JPMorganChase Gretchen WhitmerGovernor, Michigan Jim FarleyCEO, Ford
TL;DRJPMorgan Chase, Ford, and the State of Michigan have launched **Michigan LIFT** (Launchpad for Industrial Innovation and Transformation) to onshore advanced manufacturing supply chains, secure critical components, and boost domestic industrial independence.
Program Purpose: Jim...

TL;DR

JPMorgan Chase, Ford, and the State of Michigan have launched Michigan LIFT (Launchpad for Industrial Innovation and Transformation) to onshore advanced manufacturing supply chains, secure critical components, and boost domestic industrial independence.

Michigan LIFT Initiative & Supply Chain Onshoring

  • Program Purpose: Jim Farley explained that Michigan LIFT is designed to fix supply chain vulnerabilities by onshoring capabilities—such as advanced manufacturing, new technology, magnets, and rare earth elements—that were previously offshored for lower costs.
  • Ford's Commitment: Jim Farley stated that Ford is committing $1 billion in purchasing power for new Michigan-based companies building advanced manufacturing capabilities.
  • Role of Startups & Small Businesses: Jamie Dimon emphasized that innovative startups need actual orders and production-scale plants (not just experimental ones) to succeed, making initiatives like LIFT essential for bridging that gap.
  • Cross-Border Partnerships: Governor Gretchen Whitmer acknowledged Canada as a vital ally and noted that Canadian companies in the industrial and aluminum supply chains can apply to the program, highlighting that all Ford truck aluminum ingots cross the Detroit border.

Skilled Trades & Workforce Development

  • The Trades Deficit: Jim Farley characterized the U.S. skilled trades deficit as a "crisis" with 1.7 million open skilled trade jobs, noting we are currently in the "second inning" of addressing it.
  • Educational Bias: Jim Farley pointed out that high school advisors recommend 4-year degrees while only 2% recommend trade schools, despite high-quality trade schools boasting nearly 100% graduation rates.
  • State Workforce Rankings: Governor Gretchen Whitmer highlighted that Michigan is ranked #4 in the country for workforce efforts by *Site Selector Magazine* and noted the state has the highest concentration of engineers per capita in the country.
  • Debt-Free Training: Governor Gretchen Whitmer detailed Michigan's investments in free community college and accessible apprenticeships that allow workers to earn a good living without carrying student debt—jobs she noted will not be replaced by AI.

National Economic Resilience & Capital Markets

  • Connection to Military Power: Jamie Dimon argued that a powerful military is predicated on having a pre-eminent U.S. economy, which anchors the dollar as the global reserve currency.
  • JP Morgan's Broader Initiative: Jamie Dimon reported that the one-year anniversary of JPMorgan's security and resiliency initiative is approaching, noting the firm has invested $5 billion directly into non-venture, pre-public companies focused on security and resiliency.
  • Advantage of U.S. Capital Markets: Jamie Dimon contrasted vibrant U.S. venture capital, private equity, debt markets, and risk-taking capital with a stagnated Europe, while identifying government borrowing—not AI—as the primary source of capital crowding out.
  • Global Competition: Jim Farley discussed the intense speed of global competition, specifically noting that Ford developed a small group in the U.S. and California to out-innovate competitors and is rolling out a $30,000 vehicle engineered in California and Michigan and built in Louisville, Kentucky.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Jamie Dimon predicted that JPMorgan may exceed its initial $1.5 trillion goal for its security and resiliency initiative by its one-year anniversary.
  • Jim Farley claimed that Ford's upcoming $30,000 vehicle—engineered in California and Michigan and built in Louisville, Kentucky—will be fully competitive with Chinese EV giant BYD.

What does it all mean? What is it? We’re trying to fix a problem, onshoring things that were offshored in the name of pennies on the dollar to make here in the U.S. where we need it to be independent, even defend ourselves. Ford is going to commit a billion dollars of purchasing for new companies that are going to be building advanced manufacturing, new capabilities, even magnets and rare earth that we used to buy overseas and now we’re going to buy here with the help of JP Morgan as well as the state of Michigan. Why is it so important for Michigan? Why is this the right place to start this? Well, we know how to build things in Michigan; that’s who we are in our DNA. We were, you know, the arsenal of democracy. This is about homeland security; it’s about advanced manufacturing. That’s who we are in Michigan. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman Talks IPO Planning, DevDay | Bloomberg Talks

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·10:11·40 views·2026-09-29

TL;DROpenAI CEO Sam Altman discussed the company's rapid growth and product roadmap at DevDay, highlighting the launch of the new agent DOTS and the model GPT-6.1 Soul.
Altman emphasized that while OpenAI is experiencing a very steep growth period, investors are patient regarding IPO...

TL;DR

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman discussed the company's rapid growth and product roadmap at DevDay, highlighting the launch of the new agent DOTS and the model GPT-6.1 Soul. Altman emphasized that while OpenAI is experiencing a very steep growth period, investors are patient regarding IPO planning, allowing the company to prioritize safety and capability management before facing public market pressures.

Product Strategy and DOTS Agent

  • Target Audience for DOTS: Starting as a premium, prosumer, and enterprise-focused product using the Astro model, Altman noted that OpenAI will introduce a mass market consumer version over time.
  • Economic Model: Although DOTS is priced higher than other market offerings, Altman explained that the price reflects its high compute requirements, intelligence level, and capability to handle complex work.
  • Model Release Decisions: OpenAI recently held back the release of Astra 6.1 and paused training on a separate model to dedicate more attention to safety, alignment, monitoring, and security.
  • Balancing Speed and Capability: Altman highlighted that users demand speed, price, and capability simultaneously, pointing to the new ultra-fast tier as a major productivity driver despite its higher running costs.

Business Momentum and IPO Planning

  • Growth Trajectory: Altman described OpenAI as being in a "very steep growth period," citing phenomenal growth for ChatGPT across consumer, developer, and enterprise segments alongside new DevDay product launches.
  • IPO Timeline: Investors are reportedly patient with IPO planning, giving OpenAI time to establish its operational footing and navigate new safety requirements before taking on the pressures of being a newly public company.

Industry Safety and Collaboration

  • Frontier Pacing Agreement: Altman noted alignment with peers like Anthropic and Elon Musk regarding pacing the frontier, emphasizing the need for shared safety case standards.
  • Verification Methods: Suggested implementation methods include independent evaluators, government review, or peer checks to ensure companies fulfill safety commitments.
  • Public Transparency: Altman asserted that disclosing risks and benefits is a core responsibility tied to OpenAI's mission rather than a byproduct of going public.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mass Market Consumer Version: Sam Altman stated that OpenAI will make a mass market consumer version of the DOTS agent over time.

Notable Quotes

"If we can continue to drive the quality up and the price down and really lead the Pareto frontier at every point on that curve, people will use our tools in tremendous ways." — Sam Altman
"We're clearly getting into a very steep part of the capabilities curve and given that our whole mission is to build this for people... when we need to make a decision about slowing down our training or our model release so that we can make more progress and put more of our attention into safety, alignment, monitoring, security, we will happily do that." — Sam Altman

The reason that I think both those are true is the better you make AI, the more people want to use it. People are clearly willing to spend more on AI than ever before, but they want to do so much with it. So our belief is that if we can continue to drive the quality up and the price down and really lead the Pareto frontier at every point on that curve, people will use our tools in tremendous ways. We've been talking about this for a few months now, but we are clearly getting into a very steep part of the capabilities curve. Given that our whole mission is to build this for people and to empower people, and part of that is safety—part of that is why we're distributing the benefits—when we need to make a decision about slowing down our training or our model release so that we can make more progress and put more of our attention into safety, alignment, monitoring, and security, we will happily do that. Now, of course, we're going to continue to make progress, but the world should always have confidence in our safety cases, in our safety claims, and that we are going to be responsible with this technology. We don't want to do this thing where, "Oh, we need regulation, or we need our competitors to do X, Y, or Z, or we won't be safe." We are going to do this responsibly. Why is that better as a technique than releasing a more capable model? Well, I mean, they want that too. We will also do that. I think all of those things are good. You know, speed, price, and capability are certainly three of the things people ask for the most often, and I think the answer is people want all of them in different use cases. That said, UltraAstra, I think, is a really special thing. I did not appreciate how much I was going to like it and how hard it is for me to go back. Well, it's much more expensive to run, but we do charge more for it, too. So we tried to make it something that is—we can talk to Sarah about the balance between that. Yeah, I mean, I think the market will decide, but I think people will be excited about it at this price and it's reasonable for us. We want to make sure we understand how to operate in this new way and be able to make some of these decisions in front of us without the pressure of being a newly public company. OpenAI DevDay 2026 Keynote (FULL)

OpenAI(2.1M)·53:35·555.7K views·2026-09-29
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI Romain HuetExecutive, OpenAI Tejal PatwardhanExecutive, OpenAI
TL;DR"We are kicking you off with a product that we believe is going to be a whole new way to work with AI.
OpenAI announced over 20 new products and features at DevDay 2026—led by personal autonomous agents called "dots," collaborative workspace "ChatGPT spaces," new frontier models like GPT-6.1 Sol and UltraFast, and cloud-native developer tooling—aiming to transform AI from...

TL;DR: OpenAI announced over 20 new products and features at DevDay 2026—led by personal autonomous agents called "dots," collaborative workspace "ChatGPT spaces," new frontier models like GPT-6.1 Sol and UltraFast, and cloud-native developer tooling—aiming to transform AI from a conversational assistant into an active, 24/7 collaborative partner.

New User Experiences: Dots & Spaces

  • Introduction of Dots: Dots are always-on, highly capable agents integrated into ChatGPT designed to handle complex, multi-step delegated work (e.g., managing bug reports, monitoring dependencies, or prepping board decks).
    • Omnichannel Access: Beyond ChatGPT, users can interact with their dots via text messaging, direct phone calls, or voice interfaces.
    • Enterprise Specialist Dots: Virtual teammates configured with specific goals and context to scale intensive work across an entire enterprise team.
  • ChatGPT Spaces: A dedicated collaborative environment where human team members and dots can work together on shared pages, live prototypes, interactive charts, and spreadsheets.
    • Dynamic Multi-User Collaboration: Users can tag teammates and dots (e.g., dotty) directly in comments or chat threads to execute tasks like updating bar charts or generating PR fixes.

Models, Speed, & Research Breakthroughs

  • GPT-6.1 Sol: A new model balancing high intelligence with lower cost, designed for reusing context, iterating on solutions, and handling longer tasks as an everyday driver.
    • UltraFast Model: Available across API, ChatGPT, and Codex, delivering 300 tokens per second (3x the speed of standard models).
  • New Subscription Tiers:
    • Pro-500: Offers 25x the speed and access to UltraFast.
    • Pro-200: Reopened subscription providing access to all frontier models.
  • Decisions API: Enables models to respond in a fraction of a second by selecting from a predefined set of choices.
  • AI Research Intern: Tejal Patwardhan shared that OpenAI has reached its milestone of deploying an AI research intern.
    • Task Automation: Models running in continuous loops now autonomously handle over a third of day-long research tasks without human intervention.
    • Scientific & Mathematical Impact: Models have helped solve over 100 math problems open for decades, accelerate antibiotic creation, and study ancient languages.

Developer Platform & Codex in the Cloud

  • Codex in the Cloud: Romain Huet demonstrated the new cloud-native Codex harness, allowing developers to start tasks on mobile and execute them in cloud environments (e.g., rewriting an entire backend in Rust or running secure cloud scans).
  • Computer Use & API Expansion: Computer use is now available via API for building testing agents, alongside bedrock managed agents that integrate with existing applications and data.
  • OpenAI Private Tension (Z-EDR): Delivers frontier model safety without storing customer content on OpenAI servers, establishing new enterprise privacy standards.
  • API Performance Metrics: Reliability remains above 99%, with time-to-first-token reduced by 45%.

Ecosystem, Distribution, & Business Tools

  • Ecosystem Integration: 16 launch partners signed on for sign-in functionality utilizing existing ChatGPT subscription tokens, eliminating cost barriers for end users.
  • Plugin Extensions: Developers can build editors, dashboards, and workspaces directly inside ChatGPT and Codex, with simplified human review submission processes.
  • Open Source Model Access: Through a partnership with BaseTen, enterprise customers can access open-source models directly.

President Trump, Elon Musk Announce AI-Powered "America.gov"

C-SPAN(1.9M)·58:34·54.7K views·2026-09-29
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Elon MuskEntrepreneur
TL;DRPresident Trump, Elon Musk, and administration officials announced the launch of **America.gov**, an AI-powered portal designed to consolidate government services into a single interface.
The administration also previewed the redesigned 2028 U.S. passport and detailed health-sector...

TL;DR

President Trump, Elon Musk, and administration officials announced the launch of America.gov, an AI-powered portal designed to consolidate government services into a single interface. The administration also previewed the redesigned 2028 U.S. passport and detailed health-sector initiatives like TrumpRX.gov and streamlined Medicare enrollment.

Overview of America.gov and Government Modernization

  • Single Front Door Concept: President Trump introduced America.gov as an all-inclusive government website intended to eliminate the maze of tens of thousands of federal websites and forms.
  • Reduction of Bureaucratic Waste: President Trump noted that Americans currently spend over $10 billion hours per year on government paperwork, time the administration aims to return to citizens.
  • Privacy and Security Architecture: President Trump emphasized that the platform is built from the ground up with privacy in mind, utilizing advanced technology so personal information is not tracked or stored permanently after sessions end.
  • Leadership and Development: Joe Gabbia (Airbnb co-founder and Chief Design Officer of the United States) and Peter Arnt (Chief Grand Architect of the National Design Studio) were credited by President Trump with leading the design and engineering teams behind the platform.

Healthcare Initiatives: TrumpRX.gov and Medicare

  • Prescription Savings via TrumpRX.gov: Dr. Mehmet Oz (Administrator of Medicare and Medicaid Services) highlighted that TrumpRX.gov has driven prescription price reductions and negotiations, projecting $650 billion in savings over 10 years for Americans.
  • Medicare Simplification: Dr. Mehmet Oz announced that instead of relying on a printed $100 million annual book, 70 million Medicare beneficiaries will be able to use America.gov to answer guided eligibility questions, enroll directly, find in-network doctors, and compare Part D prescription drug plans side-by-side.
  • HHS Agency Integration: Dr. Mehmet Oz credited Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. for signing on as a founding agency partner to integrate health services into the new digital framework.

Passport Modernization and the 2028 Redesign

  • Digital Passport Applications: Secretary of State Marco Rubio announced that starting next year, Americans will be able to apply for a passport entirely online using Login.gov, take photos from home via mobile device, make secure payments, and track delivery status in real time.
  • The 2028 U.S. Passport: Secretary of State Marco Rubio showcased the redesigned 2028 passport featuring a larger, more prominent gold Great Seal on the cover and internal artwork depicting 250 years of American history from Lexington to the moon landing.

Artificial Intelligence and "Super Intelligence" (S.I.)

  • Renaming AI: President Trump announced an executive order officially designating artificial intelligence as "Super Intelligence" or S.I., rejecting the term "artificial" as inadequate.
  • Global AI Dominance: President Trump asserted that the U.S. is currently leading China in AI development, stated he discussed the topic with President Xi, and rejected the idea of a joint venture to protect U.S. technological secrets.
  • Cross-Agency Integration Order: President Trump announced an executive order directing every federal agency to integrate their public-facing services directly with America.gov and the administration's AI infrastructure.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Full Medicare Integration Timeline: Dr. Mehmet Oz stated that all Medicare enrollment, provider booking, and Part D comparison features will be fully operational and eligible for public use by 2027.
  • Online Passport Rollout Timeline: Secretary of State Marco Rubio stated that Americans will be able to apply for passports entirely online starting next year, with the newly redesigned 2028 passport available for delivery starting in 2028.

"Today, if you need your first passport, you started the State Department website... When something is this hard, someone is bound to make money off of it." — Secretary of State Marco Rubio

President Trump announces the launch of America.gov — 9/29/2026

CNBC Television(3.4M)·24:41·37.6K views·2026-09-29
Donald TrumpPresident, United States
TL;DRPresident Donald Trump launched America.gov, a single digital portal designed to consolidate thousands of cumbersome government websites and over 10,000 forms into a palm-sized interface, aiming to return 10 billion hours of paperwork back to Americans.
He also announced an...

TL;DR

President Donald Trump launched America.gov, a single digital portal designed to consolidate thousands of cumbersome government websites and over 10,000 forms into a palm-sized interface, aiming to return 10 billion hours of paperwork back to Americans. He also announced an executive order renaming artificial intelligence to "super intelligence" (SI) to reflect its superior capabilities and mandated federal agencies to integrate with the new platform.

Launch of America.gov and Citizen Experience

  • Single Digital Front Door: America.gov replaces tens of thousands of separate government websites, rules, and over 10,000 forms with one unified portal.
  • Ease of Use: President Trump noted that anyone who has used Grock, Gemini, or ChatGPT will instantly know how to use the platform.
  • Streamlined Services:
    • Passports: Secretary Rubio demonstrated that getting a passport—previously requiring appointments, paperwork, and months of waiting—is now reduced to a few simple clicks.
    • Name Changes & Benefits: A woman changing her name after marriage will be able to ask the platform to fill out forms, track progress, and have new social security, passport, and insurance cards mailed to her; similar simplifications apply to reserving national park campsites, choosing Medicare plans, and securing veteran benefits.
  • Privacy and Security: Built with brand-new technology designed so that personal information is not collected or stored; user conversations disappear upon leaving the session to ensure total privacy.

Team and Leadership Recognition

  • Chief Design Officer: Airbnb co-founder Joe Gibia serves as the chief design officer of the United States and was praised as the mastermind behind the product breakthroughs.
  • Chief Brand Architect: Peter Arnell was recognized as the chief brand architect.
  • Lead Engineer: Ed Corstine and the entire National Design Studio team led the technical development.
  • Initial Visionary: Elon Musk was credited with initially envisioning the transformation of government through technology and bringing in the necessary talent.
  • Cabinet and Private Sector Leaders: President Trump acknowledged cabinet members, including Secretary Rubio, and Meta President Dena Powell, who served in his first administration.

Super Intelligence (SI) and Technology Policy

  • Name Change to SI: President Trump announced an executive order officially renaming artificial intelligence to super intelligence (SI), dismissing "artificial" as inadequate for describing supreme and brilliant technology.
  • Global Leadership: Trump stated that the United States is leading China and all other nations in super intelligence by a wide margin, emphasizing that whoever wins super intelligence wins, with no viable second place.
  • Exclusion of Joint Ventures: Trump rejected the idea of a joint venture with China regarding super intelligence to prevent handing over sensitive U.S. secrets and knowledge.

Executive Actions and Legislative Impacts

  • Mandatory Agency Integration: Trump announced he will sign an executive order directing every federal government agency to integrate all public-facing services directly with America.gov as soon as possible.
  • Time Savings: Americans currently spend over 10 billion hours per year on government paperwork, time that the administration aims to return to citizens.
  • Voter ID and Citizenship Proof: The simplified system removes traditional excuses regarding the complexity of providing proof of citizenship or voter ID, bolstering support for the Save America Act.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global Adoption: "Every advanced nation in the world will copy what we've done here this morning. We believe some won't be able to because they don't have the technology to do it but they should try and do it." — President Donald Trump
  • Iran Nuclear and Oil Outcomes: Iran will not have a nuclear weapon, and "it'll be over with very, very soon," while oil prices will tumble down. — President Donald Trump

Notable Quotes

"With America.gov, the federal government no longer stands in your way and it stands only at your service." — President Donald Trump
"Instead of forcing citizens to search through the endless maze of tens of thousands of terrible government websites and rules and more than 10,000 forms, you have over 10,000 forms to do basic acts. You'll now have one front door for every single question." — President Donald Trump
"Whoever wins super intelligence wins... You're going to have a winner or you're going to have a loser. And you're probably not going to have a second place." — President Donald Trump

The $3B Company Trying to Beat Amazon Prime

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(73.8K)·14:32·7.8K views·2026-09-29
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Sean HenryCEO, Stord
TL;DR"Amazon's product really is delivery.
Stord is on track to cross $1 billion in annual revenue this year, having 10Xed the business in scale, revenue, and volume over the last four years by building a physical intelligence layer to level the playing field with Amazon Prime for the other two-thirds of e-commerce. ...
  • Stord is on track to cross $1 billion in annual revenue this year, having 10Xed the business in scale, revenue, and volume over the last four years by building a physical intelligence layer to level the playing field with Amazon Prime for the other two-thirds of e-commerce.

Scale, Growth, and Funding Milestones

  • Series F and Revenue Growth:
    • Announced a $250 million Series F earlier this year to fuel rapid scaling and future growth.
    • On track to cross $1 billion in annual revenue this year.
    • Has 10Xed the business in terms of scale, revenue, and volume over the last four years.
  • Network Volume and Reach:
    • Processes roughly 100 million packages a year.
    • Reaches about one-third of U.S. households.
    • Represents nearly $20 billion in GMV.
    • Generates around 8 billion operational data points annually.
  • Sales Win Rate and Performance Gap:
    • Sales win rates have increased from sub-10% to over 50% as brands increasingly seek this level of capability.
    • Contrast in industry economics: Average U.S. businesses deliver for $15+ per package in 5–6 business days, Amazon delivers for $5 per package in 1–2 days (or half a day), and Stord delivers at sub-$7 to $8 in 2 days (or 1 day depending on placement).

The Robotics and AI Strategy (Stord Labs)

  • Agentic Robotics and Self-Healing:
    • Shift away from "dumb robots" that fail when variables change toward agentic robotics that build real-world models.
    • Robots use brief video inputs to learn how to self-heal and get back on track when anomalies occur (e.g., a worker walking up, holding a unit for 3 seconds, and showing it how to pack).
  • The Data Foundry Advantage:
    • Announced Stord Labs alongside the Series F funding to apply robotics, AI, drones, and a data foundry that trains robotics in reverse.
    • Post-deployment training utilizes videos of live robots, enriched by Stord's software tracking exact box dimensions, weight, and placement balance.
  • Perspective on Humanoids:
    • Sean Henry: Predicts a 2x+ efficiency output in manufacturing and robotic environments over time through various robot form factors.
    • Notes that humanoids capture the most consumer attention and media buzz, but argues that a robot does not need legs to move on the ground and actually *should not* look like a human to maximize dexterity.
    • Labels humanoids as "very much stuck in the pilot phase."

Vertical Integration and Acquisitions

  • The Roll-Up Strategy:
    • Chose to vertically integrate from day one (acting as both software and physical infrastructure) because the average warehouse relied on paper-based picking, making it impossible to adopt prime-level delivery or advanced robotics without foundational changes.
  • Acquisition Efficiency and Uplift:
    • Stord's acquisitions acquire density in the network and volume while applying unit economics and software.
    • Across most deals, Stord has seen 5 to 7x or greater uplift in efficiency and profitability within extremely short time horizons.

Founder Background and The Teal Fellowship

  • Origins:
    • Founder and CEO Sean Henry started selling products online via eBay starting in 2003 (with a member account from that year).
    • Attended Georgia Tech and became a Teal Fellow at age 19 (dropping out more than a year prior to receiving the fellowship).
  • Core Lesson Learned:
    • The fellowship emphasizes building a monopoly or striving to win your category by defining your "right to win." For Stord, this focuses on the economic flywheel where getting bigger leads to better speeds, lower costs, and increased defensibility.
