For people who don't know what Stord is, could you give us a little background? Yeah, so Stord really exists to level the playing field with Amazon Prime for the other two-thirds of commerce on the internet. When we look out there, we think that Amazon's product really is delivery, and that's what all other merchants struggle to achieve. That's what keeps all of us as consumers going to their website to check out instead of independent merchants. So we really see ourselves as the physical intelligence layer combining all the fulfillment and delivery, all of the commerce software, and all of the applied robotics and AI to achieve Amazon-level delivery costs, speeds, and consumer experiences. And so for people that aren't aware, you recently had a milestone of funding and growth. Where are we at with the business? Absolutely. So, earlier this year, we announced a $250 million Series F. That was really to not only continue scaling the business rapidly, where this year we'll be crossing a billion in annual revenue, but over the last four years alone we've 10xed the business in terms of scale and revenue and volume. And so it's a lot of fueling that future growth, where there's so much of an advantage of scale. Scale drives economics; it also drives density, and that's really what builds the moat in delivery, enabling Amazon-level speeds and costs. So, continuing to press on the gas hard with scale. We also announced Stord Labs along with it, which is very much an application of all of the latest robotics, AI, and drones, but also a data foundry actually training the robotics in reverse. And so we're really excited about how all the new technology is enabling a vertically integrated industrial business like us to just keep separating our moat from the existing industry we live in. Let's get into that, because robotics is super hot right now. It's flying, like it's become the age of robotics. Why is now the time to go hard on that? Yeah, if you really look back over history, a lot of the problem with robotics is kind of the "dumb" robots where if anything goes wrong, or if any variable is changed, they can't succeed in that environment anymore. And so if you have an operationally complex environment where every minute or hour could be hundreds of thousands of dollars or more of productivity, that traditional robotic operation can be quite risky. And so now you're getting to this point of agentic robotics where you're having robotics that are building real-world models that can see videos for very brief times and learn how to self-heal and get back on track. So, let's say you get a slightly different size or form factor in a unit in a fulfillment center. Does all of a sudden that go down until it can show up and code or do something different, or does the robot learn on the fly with one worker walking up and holding it for three seconds and showing them how to put it in the box? And so, that's really where robotics is going and why now is such a moment for it. Yet at the same time, if you look back through history and if you study the Teslas and SpaceXs of the world, the whole process and equation is: how do you go from refining your processes physically and operationally to then software, and kind of repeat that loop over and over again? And only then can you really get the advancement you want in hardware because now you're applying it and really hard-coding whatever that process was. So if it was under-optimized, you're going to fall behind now in a hard-coded way. That's really the issue with robotics in today's world; a lot of the physical world out there that needs these robots doesn't have the basis of that best-in-class process or vertically integrated software to go apply the robot. And so that's kind of the chasm that robotics is going to have to cross in the period ahead, now that they are getting more agentic and self-healing as well. So what are the typical data points you are collecting through robotics that inform the software and back and forth? Yeah, for us, we process about 8 billion operational data points a year. And that could be anything from how to place a piece of inventory in the network to how to plan for certain consumer demand to how a unit is picked in the facility with labeling on the hands so that they can learn all of these different picking and packing patterns. There's also post-deployment training, which is videos of the robots after they've actually gone live to show those errors and to help them keep learning more and more. And so there's really a ton of data that goes into a business like Stord. And when we're processing about a hundred million packages a year to about a third of US households now, almost 20 billion dollars of GMV, that's really one of the most data-rich environments that all of these labs and robotics companies are trying to either simulate and learn. And that's why we have this not only head start in the e-commerce and logistics industry that we sit in, but also have such an advancement in the robotics in the early phase with these partnerships with leading companies and labs deploying across the Stord network because of that data-rich environment. Where let's say you deploy a camera to do the post-deployment training on a robot. Well, because of our software, we also know in that video what the exact dimensions and weight of the box are. Where's the weight if it's placed? So it's going to have balance difference because of the pack-out process. And when you can enrich these videos with not only labeling of the hands, but that level of vertically integrated data, the learning is just so much faster. I have to ask this, this is a selfish question because they're so popular now and creating a lot of media buzz, but do humanoid robotics belong in a factory or in a manufacturing warehouse, literally anywhere? Over time, I think that you'll see 2x plus efficiency output in manufacturing and robotic environments due to really all these different form factors of robots. And I think humanoids are the most eye-capturing when you look at a video of it as a consumer or just a human and you see this thing. That's really what gets the attention. But there's a lot of different applications of them because the history was that you could kind of either have a mounted arm that would pick things or something on the ground like an AGV that would move things—not two in one. Well, you don't actually have to have legs for a robot to move on the ground. It doesn't literally have to look like a human to have the same level of capability. It actually should not look like a human if you want to maximize dexterity. And so I think humanoids are interesting but very much stuck in the pilot phase. And you're seeing a lot of advancement in different levels of robotics as well. Turns out humans are very inefficient form factors. That might be the quote of the day. So Stord has grown by also acquisition, and roll-ups are kind of a hot thing as well. So are you a roll-up? How do you think about acquisitions? What does the growth of the business kind of pan out to in the next couple years? Yeah, when we started this business, we toured warehouses, fulfillment centers, and we were thinking about: should we build the software layer on top to orchestrate a Prime-like network for other brands? And we kind of got stuck on this problem of, well, if you walk in the average warehouse, many of them are still doing paper-based picking where a worker prints out, "go build an order of these units," and walks around the facility either from memory or from just looking around at cards and aisles in the facility. So how can you really expect Prime-level delivery, drones, and all the advancement in robotics if the industry they're going into is so far behind? So from the earliest day, we really chose to vertically integrate and say, "Well, we're going to be both the software and the physical behind this to power that Prime-like delivery for everyone else." So that when we build this best-in-class software, we can actually apply it to the physical service it's behind. And that's really what's changed. Back when we started, physical wasn't really in vogue; it wasn't the thing to do. And now you're seeing these new applied kind of roll-up companies, holding companies, and more saying, "Wait a second, I can build the best accounting software or whatever it may be, but if the accounting industry is slow to adopt, what if I go accrue all that advantage into my own company and my own accounting service where I own the data, the customer, and the software and AI powering this?" And so really, if you think about Stord, our secret sauce is this vertically integrated software, but how it's applied to real-world operations and the flywheel of this business is that the more volume you serve, the lower cost, the faster speed, and the greater trust. And if the average business out in the US is delivering for 15-plus dollars a package in five to six business days, while Amazon's delivering for $5 a package in one to two days—if not even half a day—that's the fundamental gap that exists. And so the race to scale and spin that flywheel to level the playing field is really on, and we're in the lead. And so when we look at acquisitions, we've actually been probably the most efficient on cost and also the highest outcome on uplift to these businesses because you're buying both density in the network and volume, but you're also applying your unit economics and your software throughout this business. And so in most of the deals we've done, we've seen five to seven or even more times uplift of the efficiency and therefore profitability of these businesses in extremely short time horizons. Damn. So you're a Thiel Fellow at 19. That's right. You're not 19 anymore. Not 19 anymore. It's about 10 years ago. Okay. You're now evolved and mature. So you started in logistics there. Like, if you ask any consumer, "What's the best delivery service? Where do you want to shop online?" The answer is Amazon. Well, why is no one doing that for the other two-thirds of the internet across e-commerce? What was the biggest lesson you learned from the Thiel Fellowship? I think they really impress upon you what is your right to win and essentially build the biggest company possible, i.e., build a monopoly over time. And I think that their whole goal is that that's really what you should strive for, not that it should exist in the sense you want to win your category. And so I think they really dug into that precision of: why will this win in the long term? And as we've spun that flywheel, we can trace our win rate in sales from sub 10% to over 50%, because these brands can't find that level of capability elsewhere. And so when you're able to articulate why as you get bigger, you actually get better as a company, and it becomes harder to compete with you, and you can move faster at scale—that's why we're growing faster at scale. I think it actually accrues to the application layer and to the real world. And so I believe humans' lives are going to get better. I believe physical businesses like Stord are going to win and get faster and cheaper, and long term, it's actually these super fast-growth AI companies and robotics that end up commoditized by how low the cost becomes to replicate them internally across companies and for individuals even. The Defender's Window at enterprise scale with Standard Chartered

OpenAI(2.1M)·13:14·17.1K views·2026-09-29
Matt WeaverHead of Applied AI Architects, OpenAI Reuben AthaideHead of Innovation, Applications & Assurance, Standard Chartered
TL;DR- **Understanding Exploits over Vulnerabilities:** Reuben Athaide (**Standard Chartered**) noted that frontier models shorten the time to understand software and identify exploits, making it crucial for global financial institutions to understand exploitable paths faster than attackers.
Standard Chartered is leveraging OpenAI Daybreak to outpace attackers by shifting security from static code analysis to contextual vulnerability assessment across enterprise microservices, architectures, and trust boundaries. By embedding AI remediation directly into the...

TL;DR: Standard Chartered is leveraging OpenAI Daybreak to outpace attackers by shifting security from static code analysis to contextual vulnerability assessment across enterprise microservices, architectures, and trust boundaries. By embedding AI remediation directly into the development pipeline, the global bank is transforming security teams from blockers into enablers.

The Defender's Window and Frontier AI at Enterprise Scale

  • Understanding Exploits over Vulnerabilities: Reuben Athaide (Standard Chartered) noted that frontier models shorten the time to understand software and identify exploits, making it crucial for global financial institutions to understand exploitable paths faster than attackers.
    • Attackers focus on attack paths across systems, APIs, IDs, and trust boundaries rather than isolated repos and assets.
    • The goal is moving beyond generating a longer list of CVEs to identifying what is truly exploitable.
  • Early Adoption of OpenAI Daybreak: Standard Chartered is among the first banks globally to evaluate OpenAI Daybreak to gain insights traditional security approaches miss.
    • Matt Weaver (OpenAI) highlighted Daybreak as a critical component of the upcoming defender ecosystem.

The Power of Context in Code Security

  • Context Over Model Choice: According to Reuben Athaide, having comprehensive context matters more than which specific model is selected.
  • Inputs Required for Deep Context: To achieve actionable findings, Standard Chartered feeds additional data into the model alongside codebases, including:
    • Architecture diagrams and architecture deployment models.
    • Dependencies and connected services utilized across assets.
    • The original solution intent of the item being reviewed.
  • Uncovering Hidden Gaps: Using Daybreak, the bank discovered areas and insights that traditional tooling entirely failed to pick up on.

Cross-Functional Collaboration and Developer Trust

  • Collaborative Validation: Reuben Athaide emphasized that simply showing demos to development teams is insufficient; findings must be validated against actual code, architecture deployments, and environments through cross-functional groups combining application security and development teams.
  • Shifting Left and Self-Service: Security is being integrated earlier into the pipeline, creating virtual workspace areas in the development cycle where teams can test and verify exploitability safely.
  • Efficiency in Remediation: Instead of adding unverified tasks to a developer backlog or acting as a restrictive gatekeeper, Standard Chartered uses frontier AI to help developers understand root causes and directly automate remediation.

Foundation Readiness and Global Scale

  • Strengthening Core Foundations: Reuben Athaide stressed that organizations must establish solid foundations before deploying AI, which will otherwise amplify existing flaws:
    • Reduce technical debt and technology complexities (such as minimizing the number of programming languages used).
    • Keep architectures up-to-date to provide necessary context.
    • Maintain solid identity and access control mechanisms.
  • Managing a Global Footprint: While operating across numerous global markets and regulatory regimes, Standard Chartered concentrates its development teams in a few key hubs to streamline coordination, aiming for a developer self-serve model with appropriate guardrails.
  • Scaling for Blast Radius Analysis: Looking ahead, Reuben Athaide noted the necessity of running scans in bulk within sandboxed environments rather than repo-by-repo, enabling the bank to evaluate connected applications and understand the full blast radius.

Notable Quotes

*"It isn't really about finding vulnerabilities in code, but it's about understanding what is actually exploitable because attackers at the end of the day, they aren't looking at repos and assets... They look at attack paths across systems, across APIs, across IDs, and across trust boundaries."* — Reuben Athaide
*"AI when it's released into an environment it'll it'll amplify whatever it sees right and the the organizations that have the right foundation and if it's solid they'll be able to make the most out of it."* — Reuben Athaide
Ruben, we've heard a lot today about the defenders window, so I'd love to start the conversation there. What does it mean for Standard Chartered and how you're protecting your applications? Right. I mean, for Standard Chartered, I guess with frontier models, what we've seen is that it obviously makes the time to understand software and the time to figure out and identify exploits a lot shorter. As a bank at a global scale, you need to be able to understand what these exploits are a lot faster than the attackers. So, it isn't really about finding vulnerabilities in code, but it's about understanding what is actually exploitable because attackers, at the end of the day, they aren't looking at repos and assets, which is what a lot of security programs look at. They look at attack paths across systems, across APIs, across IDs, and across trust boundaries. So, that's really key. Got it. I couldn't agree more that it's not just about adding to a longer list of CVEs. Would it be able to understand what's happening across different repositories, what's happening across architectures and trust boundaries? So, that's why we started looking into this. What we found initially—our earlier observations—are that when the system is able to understand across architecture, you know, we have a lot of microservices, so across microservices and repositories, what exactly is going on, you get a lot more findings. Context really matters; it is more important to have that context than which model you pick because with that context, you're able to find a lot of things that are truly exploitable. What else are you feeding into the model at the time that you give it the codebase to help it do this? So, things like the architecture diagram, what is the architecture deployment model of what we're looking at, what are the dependencies, what are other services that are connected or being used by this asset, and what was the solution intent of this item? Are there any lessons you learned about what worked well, or how did you win them over or get them to start to adopt this way of thinking into the way that they're building products and services inside the bank? It's not just focusing on, "Hey, we found something." It's about how do we get better efficiency on remediation and getting the right security outcomes. One, them understanding the root cause of why something happened means they may do it differently next time around and won't build their applications that way. The other is not trying to call it out, but working with them to remediate. Also, using the frontier AI to do the remediation so it's not just about another dashboard, right? Yeah, and not just adding another task to the backlog. I think, coming back to the defenders window for a second, there's a sense of urgency which we certainly feel. I'm sure that many in this room also feel a sense of urgency to address the challenge in front of us. But an organization like Standard Chartered is large, has processes, and has governance which maybe isn't designed to move at the speed that we're now trying to. Historically, security has always been looked at as a gatekeeper or a blocker. It's just trying to change that mindset to be more enablers. Whatever we build and do, it's integrated into the pipeline. One of the key things is to have the developers have access to that earlier. It's like shifting left and allowing them to have access to that earlier. Some of the things that you spoke about this afternoon resonated, like having a sort of virtual workspace in their development cycle where they can test this out as it's running and know for a fact that this is something that's exploitable. They see the evidence that it's exploitable and then they prioritize based on exploits. When they know that you are trying to help them and enable them to go faster safely, you build that trust. I think it goes hand-in-hand with providing them with agentic coding and tools to accelerate the work and make sure it's not a huge traditional burden. Having worked in the security space for a very long time, as we were just discussing before we came on stage, there's always been this race between defenders and threat actors. This moment, though, feels like it requires thinking differently. How are you thinking differently than you were five years ago about the security space, and what do you think other security leaders in this room should be thinking differently to rise to the moment? To be quite honest, a few things that we still need to do, and it's quite evident when you go through and do this testing, is that you have to make sure that you've got your foundations down right. Focus on your foundations. Try to make sure that any technical debt is reduced. Reduce any technology complexities—like, not having too many languages. Make sure that your architecture is up to date because that will enable you to have the right context to provide, and have a solid identity and access control. Ultimately, AI when it's released into an environment will amplify whatever it sees, and the organizations that have the right foundation will be able to make the most out of it. That makes total sense. Is there anything that's been surprising as you've been on this journey over the last six months or so adopting GenAI into the security processes? Any learnings or lessons that you weren't expecting that you think others might benefit from through that experience? The most part has been about that context and how important that is. It leads you to some of the things that you take for granted in the SDLC that you think development teams are doing, like keeping the architecture up to date, and then you find there are gaps in that, so that has to get addressed. That has been a little bit of a surprise. One of the things that's been comforting is to know that there's this push asking, "Hey, have you found something that is out of the ordinary?" There hasn't been a lot of that. What we found resonates; we expect some of those things to be there, but there are certain areas that traditional tooling did not pick up on, and that's been the key thing we've been able to pick up with this method. That makes sense. One of the fascinating things for me about working with Standard Chartered is that you operate in so many markets around the world, so many regulatory regimes, and therefore many different systems. TL;DR

Deel Co-Founder and CRO Shuo Wang breaks down how the global payroll and HR platform scaled from $1M to $100M ARR in 20 months, and from $100M to $1B ARR in just three years, by operating globally from day one, applying systems engineering principles to remote sales, and maintaining an unrelenting hustle.

Company Scale, Metrics & Global Operations

  • Staggering Growth Trajectory:
    • Revenue Milestones: Grew from $1M to $100M in ARR in 20 months; scaled from $100M to $1B in ARR in 3 years; currently surpasses $1.5B in revenue with a $17.3B valuation.
    • Global Reach: Live in 160 different countries, maintaining in-house compliance, payroll, tax, and legal entities in every single country.
  • Day-One Global Strategy:
    • International Roots: Co-founders Shuo Wang (born in China, moved to the US at 16) and Alex Bouaziz (born in France, moved to the US at 19) designed Deel to be international from the start.
    • Talent Empowerment: Built a system enabling people worldwide to find jobs regardless of location rather than concentrating solely on a single domestic market like standard Silicon Valley playbooks.
  • High-Profile Customer Base:
    • Enterprise & AI Clients: Trusted by companies including Eleven Labs, retail brands Chanel and Hermes, major banks, venture capitalists, and the host podcast *Sorcery*.

Sales Machine & Remote Organization

  • Engineering Approach to Revenue:
    • Systems Thinking: As an MIT-trained robotics engineer and former CTO, Shuo applies scientific, systems-level design to sales by aligning client needs with clear sales team incentives.
  • Distributed Sales Infrastructure:
    • Scale and Culture: Manages sales teams across 120 different countries while maintaining a remote-first culture centered on competitive commission structures and a winning mindset.
    • Deel Kickoff: An annual five-day internal event bringing together close to 1,500 team members to align on new-year goals, product focus areas, and company strategy.
  • Founder Sales Philosophy:
    • The Core Hot Take: Shuo asserts that founders simply aren't selling enough, lacking hustle, and failing to push aggressively enough for revenue and clients.

Marketing Activations & Founder Dynamics

  • Viral Marketing & Snoop Dogg:
    • Global Partnership: Deel partnered with Snoop Dogg to help him hire global ice cream tasters at a compensation rate of $10,000 per month, proving that even major brands must think globally.
  • Co-Founder Complementarity:
    • Alex Bouaziz (CEO): Operates with a "big brother" mentality; highly visionary, product-focused, and obsessed with customer service and product quality.
    • Shuo Wang (CRO): Focuses on numbers, data sets, execution, and operational performance.
  • Inspirations & Mentors:
    • James Dyson: Inspired Shuo's first robotics company through his obsession with solving specific engineering problems and perfecting simple ideas (from vacuum cleaners to a $500 toothbrush).
    • Jeff Bezos: Serves as an ongoing inspiration for scale through his system thinking, visionary execution, and belief that a founder's biggest competitor is themselves.

There's Existential Risk Of AI Agents 'Not Aligning' with Their Humans, Says Gensler

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·12:56·3.1K views·2026-09-29
Gary GenslerFormer Chair, SEC Tom KeeneHost, Bloomberg Podcasts Paul SweeneyHost, Bloomberg Podcasts
TL;DR"The capital that our capital markets is hovering on top of, uh, is an investment boom.
Former SEC Chair Gary Gensler warns that the AI investment boom—double the relative size of the 1990s internet bubble—faces severe structural challenges from Chinese open-weight models, existential safety risks of misaligned AI agents, and systemic vulnerabilities in the...

TL;DR: Former SEC Chair Gary Gensler warns that the AI investment boom—double the relative size of the 1990s internet bubble—faces severe structural challenges from Chinese open-weight models, existential safety risks of misaligned AI agents, and systemic vulnerabilities in the retail-heavy private credit market.

The AI Infrastructure Boom and the Chinese Competitive Challenge

  • Scale of the AI Investment:
    • The current capital market investment boom hovering over AI (including data centers) accounts for 2.5% of the US economy, roughly twice the relative size of the internet tech boom in the 1990s overseen by Alan Greenspan (which was 1% to 1.5%).
    • US firms like Anthropic and OpenAI have pulled in users rapidly, but are spending massive amounts of capital raised through equity and fixed income markets.
  • The Chinese Open-Weight Threat:
    • Chinese AI models may be slightly behind at the frontier (roughly 2 to 6 months behind US models), but they are spending significantly less capital.
    • Many US companies are considering using cheaper Chinese models (such as open-weight models) for reasoning tasks instead of OpenAI or Anthropic, directly undermining the Western revenue proposition and making it harder to justify massive AI spending.
  • Regulatory Challenges and "Jailbreaks":
    • Implementing guardrails is difficult because threat actors can strip security measures off models (analogous to removing a catalytic converter from an automobile).
    • Neither the current administration nor Congress has institutionalized ex ante guardrails effectively yet, though Congress is beginning to lean into the issue.

AI Capabilities, Misalignment, and Existential Risk

  • Autonomous Agent Risks:
    • Advanced AI capabilities include writing its own software, self-replicating, and deploying agents across the internet.
    • OpenAI incident: OpenAI released a report detailing an incident this summer where AI agents automatically multiplied to 1,200 different agents simultaneously searching for vulnerabilities—and successfully found them at Hugging Face.
  • Alignment and Deception:
    • Models increasingly display reasoning regarding how to deceive humans and escape guarded sandboxes, presenting existential risks of AI agents not aligning with their human users.
  • Systemic Financial Risks:
    • If financial institutions widely adopt similar AI models, it could lead to herd behavior and trigger systemic risk across the financial sector.
    • On the consumer side, AI tools like Facebook Muse might help consumers find higher-rate deposits or move into money market funds, potentially causing deposit flight for traditional banks (though slower than predicted in Apollo's Torsten Sløk note).

Private Credit, Retailization, and Market Vulnerabilities

  • Mismatch in Private Credit:
    • Retail investors (including high-net-worth retail publics) are being sold illiquid private credit products paired with promises of liquidity.
    • True high returns require locking up risk capital for longer periods without redemptions.
  • The Retrenchment Threat:
    • While the "retailization" of private credit is working currently, a market retrenchment is a matter of "when, not if."
    • When the AI and data center debt complex hits a downturn and the tide goes out, retail investors holding paper worth $0.80 or $0.90 on the dollar (or lower) will demand an exit, exposing significant structural risk.

Political Landscape and Responsible Capitalism

  • Transitions in US Politics:
    • Both political parties in the US and Europe are experiencing transitions driven by rapid economic changes, AI disruption, and the US stepping back from world-stage leadership.
  • The Case for Responsible Capitalism:
    • Gensler emphasizes a deep belief in responsible capitalism, pointing to Franklin Roosevelt and Congress establishing SEC investor protection guardrails 90 years ago as a precedent for managing modern technological disruptions.

Notable Quotes

"There are existential risk about AI agents misaligned or not aligned with their human, uh, um, users... that threat actors will use it and it will disrupt markets." — Gary Gensler

Traders Have Data, Fedspeak and AI to Navigate | Bloomberg Surveillance

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·36:19·1.2K views·2026-09-29
Tom KeeneHost, Bloomberg Surveillance Paul SweeneyHost, Bloomberg Surveillance Gary GenslerMIT Professor and former SEC Chair
TL;DRFinancial markets navigate an unprecedented AI investment boom and persistent inflation amid geopolitical pressures, prompting debate over the safety of retailizing private credit and the appeal of intermediate bond yields.
Scale of the Investment Boom: Former SEC Chair...

TL;DR

Financial markets navigate an unprecedented AI investment boom and persistent inflation amid geopolitical pressures, prompting debate over the safety of retailizing private credit and the appeal of intermediate bond yields.

AI Investment Boom and Systemic Market Risks

  • Scale of the Investment Boom:
    • Former SEC Chair Gary Gensler notes the AI capital markets trade is about twice the size of the 1990s internet boom, accounting for 2.5% of the US economy (compared to 1.0% to 1.5% for internet infrastructure).
  • Competitive Pressures from China:
    • Chinese firms utilizing "openweight models" provide cheaper reasoning alternatives, undercutting revenue propositions for US leaders like Anthropic and OpenAI despite lower spending in the Chinese economy.
  • Guard Rails and Security Risks:
    • AI agents exhibit autonomous vulnerability testing, as seen in an OpenAI incident where agents multiplied to 1,200 simultaneous agents finding flaws at Hugging Face.
    • Gensler warns that threat actors can remove AI guard rails similarly to removing a catalytic converter from an automobile.
  • Systemic Financial Risks:
    • Apollo’s Torsten Sløk highlights that AI consumer tools could accelerate deposit flight from traditional banks into higher-yielding alternatives.
    • Gensler warns that uniform model usage across finance risks dangerous herding behavior and systemic exposure when the current AI debt-laden data center boom retrenches.

Fixed Income Opportunities and Credit Strategy

  • Bond Yield Attractiveness:
    • George Bory (Allspring Global Investments) points out that nominal yields crossing 5% in Treasuries and offering up to 6% (such as Microsoft's 22-year AAA bonds) provide compelling real returns, supported by the expectation that inflation will normalize over a 5 to 10-year horizon.
  • Duration Positioning:
    • Recommends intermediate duration, defining the belly of the curve between 5 and 10 years (specifically 4 to 6 duration), avoiding the excessive price volatility of 30-year bonds.
  • Municipal Bonds:
    • Bory favors municipal bonds for their tax-equivalent yields, robust credit fundamentals, and steady tax-base backstops independent of geopolitical shocks.

Consumer Affordability and the K-Shaped Economy

  • Household Cost Pressures:
    • Meredith Whitney (Meredith Whitney Advisory Group) notes that property insurance costs are up 73% since the pandemic.
    • Citing a Brown University Watson Group tracker, the cost of the Iran war equates to a $900 increase per household, forcing lower-income consumers to cut grocery spending (flat to negative year-to-date) to afford gasoline as prices cross $4.00.
  • Banking Sector Dynamics:
    • Whitney states US banks are lending primarily to non-revolvers (prime/superprime) and non-deposit financial institutions (private credit) rather than struggling retail consumers, making major US banks less attractive than cheaper European alternatives.

Wealth Transfer and Private Wealth Management

  • Evolution of Advisory Services:
    • Onur Erzan (Alliance Bernstein) emphasizes that younger generations inheriting baby boomer wealth bypass traditional 60/40 asset allocations in favor of holistic, multi-generational planning covering taxes, philanthropy, and family governance.
  • Role of AI in Wealth Management:
    • Erzan views AI as an enabler and operational scaler for advisors rather than a replacement for human judgment and trust in handling ultra-high-net-worth family needs.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Inflation and Bond Horizon — George Bory predicts that inflation will drop back down to target and normal levels over the next 5 to 10 years.
  • AI Market Retrenchment — Gary Gensler predicts that a retrenchment in the AI trade will occur, causing data center debt in the private credit complex to drop below 100 cents on the dollar (trading at 80 to 90 cents or lower), exposing retail investors to liquidity mismatches.

Notable Quotes

"Bond yields in treasuries broke above 5% and dragged other bonds along with it... once you get to certain yield levels, good things tend to happen to bond investors." — George Bory
"When the tide goes out, you see who's swimming with their trunks off." — Gary Gensler (quoting Warren Buffett)
"Regardless of what's happening in the Middle East or in Ukraine or in any other geopolitical hot spot there's a good chance you're going to flush your toilet today. And so municipalities get paid when you flush your toilet." — George Bory

Who in God's name is going to institute the guard rails? I don't see any institution with motivations to on an ex-ante basis get out front on AI technology. Where is it? Well, currently it's not in this administration nor in the Congress, though I would say the Congress is starting to really lean into it. But look, Tom, we put guard rails on our capital markets so that those capital markets work better. And they're not perfect, but when the Securities and Exchange Commission was created, it was about investor protection. It was about making sure that the markets worked better, created the public good, that you could trust the markets, right? So, I would say on AI, it's possible. Well, I'm in the classroom teaching artificial intelligence and money, which is addressing those very questions with some terrific MIT students, and Simon and I of course talk about it repeatedly on our "Power and Consequences" podcast. My thought is three things. One is, for consumers, they might actually get some better automated access to higher rate deposits. That's what Apollo's Mr. Slok was pointing out. Trump and AI Tech Executives Speak to Reporters After Meeting

C-SPAN(1.9M)·35:06·65.7K views·2026-09-29
Donald TrumpPresident, United States Elon MuskEntrepreneur Mike JohnsonSpeaker of the House
TL;DRPresident Trump, House Speaker Mike Johnson, and tech executives (including Elon Musk, Jensen Huang, Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, Mark Zuckerberg, and Sundar Pichai) announced a White House accord committing the industry to self-regulation, safety…
controls, and community...

TL;DR

President Trump, House Speaker Mike Johnson, and tech executives (including Elon Musk, Jensen Huang, Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, Mark Zuckerberg, and Sundar Pichai) announced a White House accord committing the industry to self-regulation, safety controls, and community partnerships as they build out superintelligence infrastructure.

White House Accord & Industry Self-Regulation

  • Official Renaming: President Trump announced that artificial intelligence (AI) has officially been renamed "SI" (Super Intelligence), calling "AI" a fake or inappropriate word.
  • The Accord: Tech leaders signed an accord establishing industry commitments to safety, robust internal controls, and detection mechanisms for issues.
  • Tiered Auditing & Oversight: Mark Zuckerberg detailed the multi-layered oversight framework agreed upon:
      • Internal risk reviews conducted by each company.
      • External auditors and evaluators assessing the technology.
      • Independent reviews of audit reports by each company's Board of Directors.
  • Self-Policing vs. Guardrails: President Trump rejected government slowdowns or heavy federal guardrails, asserting that the industry will successfully self-police and watch over one another.

Economic Growth, Jobs, & Global Competition

  • Scale of Buildout: Jensen Huang described the movement as the largest industrial revolution and single largest infrastructure buildout in human history, creating chip plants, computer factories, and a million U.S. jobs alongside hundreds of billions in venture capital.
  • Global Race: President Trump emphasized that the U.S. is leading China and Europe significantly in SI infrastructure, warning that whoever wins SI wins the world, and that China or European nations would eagerly take any data centers the U.S. rejects.
  • The Future of Labor: Elon Musk predicted an "age of abundance" characterized by "universal high income" rather than just basic income, comparing job evolution to the historical shift away from human "computers" who did manual calculations.

Data Centers & Community Partnerships

  • Community Benefits: President Trump and Speaker Mike Johnson emphasized that data center developers will form direct partnerships with local communities, providing financial support such as funding for school teachers (e.g., Meta paying Richland Parish teachers over $50,000 each).
  • Energy and Grid Protections: Speaker Johnson highlighted the House's recent codification of the Ratepayer Protection Act, ensuring tech companies produce their own energy resources, put power back on the grid, and protect local ratepayers.
  • Location Strategy: Trump stated he wants data centers built inside America rather than driven abroad to places like Finland, provided they are located properly within communities to lower taxes and raise incomes.

Political Context & Border Enforcement

  • Midterms and Party Unity: Speaker Johnson stated that House Republican candidates are actively campaigning on maintaining America's technological edge over China and balancing community concerns. President Trump asserted that the Republican party is unified and that he won 21 out of 22 swing areas.
  • Immigration & Enforcement: Trump defended ICE enforcement actions, attributing low crime and poverty numbers to removing violent criminals who entered through the prior administration's open border policies.

Notable Quotes

*"It's almost like a constitution in a way... and the biggest people in the world signed that and I signed it as president and it really is a form of protection."* — President Donald Trump
*"There's no conflict between innovation, technology, and safety. We are going to have to create new technologies to advance the capabilities of AI, but we're also creating new technologies to advance the safety of AI."* — Jensen Huang
*"Trust is earned. It's not automatic. And they understand that they're earning the trust."* — Speaker Mike Johnson
*"By far the most likely outcome is an age of abundance where we don't have just universal basic income, we have universal high income."* — Elon Musk

Vertically Integrated, Horizontally Open | AI Factory Insider Ep 5

NVIDIA(2.2M)·39:13·7.5K views·2026-09-29
Kaushik ShirhattiVP AI Factory, NVIDIA Pradeep GuptaVP Industries Solutions Architecture, NVIDIA Stephen JonesCUDA Architect, NVIDIA
TL;DRNVIDIA's accelerated computing platform combines vertical integration for maximum hardware-software performance with a horizontally open ecosystem across 17 industry verticals, all powered by the 20-year-old CUDA platform and its 900+ CUDA-X libraries.
Vertical Integration...

TL;DR

NVIDIA's accelerated computing platform combines vertical integration for maximum hardware-software performance with a horizontally open ecosystem across 17 industry verticals, all powered by the 20-year-old CUDA platform and its 900+ CUDA-X libraries.

Architectural Philosophy: Vertically Integrated & Horizontally Open

  • Vertical Integration (Accelerated Performance): Designed as a full-stack computing problem to drive maximum performance through extreme hardware-software co-design.
      • *Hardware-Software Continuity:* Hardware and software co-evolve across generations (from Hopper to Blackwell to Vera Rubin, while maintaining support back to Ampere and Turing).
      • *Layered Architecture:* Core foundational layers (like `cuDNN`, `cuBLAS`, and `CUTLASS`) remain common across workloads, minimizing reinvention.
  • Horizontal Openness (Ecosystem Flexibility): Open at every layer to hardware vendors, middleware, orchestration software, model builders, and domain-specific library developers.
      • *Definition of "Open":* Freely available to use without asking permission from NVIDIA; permits building proprietary IP and businesses on top.
      • *Open Standards & Open Source:* Follows open standards (e.g., C++) and contributes actively to open-source projects (submitting code to PyTorch; open-sourcing libraries, Nemotron models, and datasets).

Industry Applications & Workloads

  • Multi-Industry Breadth: A single toolset with emergent behaviors powers diverse applications across 17 industry verticals.
  • Concrete Industry Use Cases:
      • *Healthcare & Biopharma:* Automated data processing in hospitals, image reconstruction for MRI/CT machines, and AI-driven drug discovery (reducing timelines from 10 years to under 2 years via libraries like `BioNeMo` and `cuEquivariance`).
      • *Retail:* Recommendation systems, search, and indexing (`RecSys` engine).
      • *Manufacturing:* Physics simulations.
      • *Finance:* Fraud detection, alpha signal discovery, portfolio optimization (`cuDF` for data processing, `cuGraph` for contextual relationship mapping).
      • *Smart Cities:* Intelligent infrastructure solutions (`Metropolis`).

CUDA & CUDA-X Libraries: The Foundation

  • Role of CUDA: The magic connecting applications to GPU hardware, functioning as a deep platform stack from top-level frameworks down to bottom-level hardware controllers.
  • Evolution & History:
      • Celebrates its 20th anniversary (version 1.0 launched 18+ years ago).
      • Early debugging limitations led architect Stephen Jones to write the universal `printf` function for CUDA in his first week.
      • Performance debugging began via a manual Excel spreadsheet tracking counters, which ultimately led to the creation of NVIDIA's visual profiler.
  • CUDA vs. CUDA-X Libraries:
      • *CUDA:* The underlying platform/machine.
      • *CUDA-X Libraries:* Reusable pieces of IP, data patterns, and optimized software (900+ libraries total, built by both NVIDIA and partners, including `cuBLAS`, `cuFFT`, `cuRAND`, `Riva`, and `MONAI`).
      • *Interoperability:* Lower-level and higher-level libraries interlock (e.g., `cuDNN` depends on `cuBLAS`, which depends on `CUTLASS`), allowing classical scientific computing mechanisms to augment seamlessly with AI disciplines.

Agentic AI & CUDA Skills

  • Agentic Software Development: Agents operate with a brain-like harness (context, observation, reasoning, action) and rely on tools and sub-agents.
  • CUDA-X Libraries as Agent Skills: Every CUDA-X library is being exposed as a "skill" for AI agents (e.g., exposing `cuDF` data-processing pipelines or visual profiler debugging tools to agents).
  • Impact on Developer Jobs:
      • Software engineers are shifting from writing manual code to writing prompts and acting as "agents managers."
      • Agents serve as a force multiplier, enabling developers to accomplish significantly more by pulling in domain-specific libraries automatically.
      • Deep domain expertise and full-stack system design skills remain critical for guiding agents and deploying systems efficiently.

Notable Quotes

*"The secret of CUDA is that it's not one thing. It's many, many things. It's all these different layers... But the magic is that whatever way you touch it, CUDA is the thing that is bringing you through to the underlying GPU hardware."* — Stephen Jones
*"When we say open, open really does not mean it's open source and open does not mean it's a vendor neutral... Open means it's a freely available you can go take it and use it without asking any permissions from NVIDIA and more interestingly if you go and build a business on the top of that that's completely your IP and your business."* — Pradeep Gupta
*"I must admit that I haven't written code mostly this year, because I now write prompts, because the agents write the code. And I think pretty much everyone I know in software is working the same way. Your agents are your team."* — Stephen Jones
Welcome to AI Factory Insider. My name is Kaushik Shirhatti and I'm your host. In the previous episodes, we talked about what an enterprise AI factory is, we talked about enterprise reference architectures, we talked about NVIDIA's AI factory, we looked at agentic AI, and we looked at tokenomics. As Lindsey, my partner in crime, and I were reviewing these episodes, there are a couple of themes that kept coming up which we felt that we didn't take the time to stop and explain, so we decided to make an episode out of it. Today we are going to talk about two very important topics. One is the concept of vertically integrated, horizontally open AI factories and how they are changing the arc of different industries with AI. And the second thing is that one thing that is powering all of this—the agents, the tokens, the reference architectures, the factories itself—and that's CUDA. I couldn't think of a better guest than I have today, so I wanted to introduce our guests: Stephen Jones, who is one of our main architects for CUDA, and Pradeep Gupta, who is our vice president of industry solutions. So, welcome, guys. Thank you. So let's get started. Pradeep, I'm going to start with you. Let's start with the concept of vertically integrated, horizontally open. What does that mean? First of all, thank you, Kaushik, for having both of us on this show; it's exciting times to be here. I think NVIDIA is a platform computing company. The way we have designed our platform, it's open, modular, and flexible. When we say we are vertically integrated—and we also sometimes use the term vertically architected and horizontally open—it simply means that when we say vertically integrated, we're designing it for performance. Think of the way we are thinking about AI, which is a full-stack computing problem; we are thinking about everything coming together and integrated so that we can drive for maximum performance. That's how our reference architectures are coming up. When we say horizontally open, it means that at every layer in this platform, in this stack, we can open it up to all our ecosystem partners. We believe that we don't need to do everything by ourselves. We have big ecosystem partners, whether that's from our hardware vendors, from our middleware, from the orchestration and management software companies, from the model builders, or from the people who are developing domain-specific libraries. We want to open each and every layer to our ecosystem so that everyone can be a part of this entire platform, which is what we call an accelerated computing platform. Yeah, that's a great definition because I feel like in the previous episode, we spent a lot of time talking about vertical integration, the optimization of performance, and the tokens. But the horizontally open part is like all of our partners, all of our ISVs, our ecosystem, and ultimately all of our partners in our enterprise organizations. That gives them the flexibility to use their own data and their own workloads. Stephen, you've been around for a long, long time, and you've seen industries evolve with AI. What are some of the most interesting industries and some of the most interesting use cases that you're seeing today? So I think, in some way, what surprises me the most—but also the real magic—is how a single set of tools has such broad applicability. That's especially true for AI with emergent behaviors, the idea that you can train a model on the English language on the internet, and it can be used for drug discovery, finance, gaming, and all sorts of different things. One of the best things when you're an engineer is when you build something and people use it in a way you never even imagined. That happens all the time because it turns out to be such a general tool, and the sheer breadth of the different ways people apply it just amazes me every single day. That's awesome. Pradeep, you have one of the widest apertures of anyone I know. You personally and your team are talking to all different enterprise verticals, looking at all different use cases, and giving that feedback back to our product engineering teams. What are you seeing when you think about different industries today? Yeah, I think that's a very interesting part of my job. We have 17 industry verticals through which we go to market. There are so many exciting use cases. Think of a hospital or a medical system; there is a lot of automated data processing which can be done through AI. If you go into biopharma, AI-driven drug discovery is really picking up; how can you take a drug that takes ten years to design and do it in two years or maybe even less? In retail, they are always thinking about what is the next product to recommend to customers, so recommendation systems, searching, and indexing have become very popular. If you go into manufacturing, simulation is a big piece that you can run on the platform. In medical devices, MRI or CT machines or image reconstruction algorithms are running on this platform. There's almost each and every industry, and I don't want to miss finance, where you have so much fraud detection. You can always find that alpha signal among all the noise in the data you are getting to really find out how you think about your portfolio optimization problems. Yeah, that's interesting because when we think about industries, you think about one company, but even within a retail giant, there are literally ten or fifteen different use cases depending on the business unit. It could be retail, consumer-facing, security, or operations. As you think about these horizontally open and vertically integrated factories, what stays the same and what is different when you think about different use cases? I think that's a very interesting way to think about it, and that's how you can think of how we actually designed our platform and how our partners are integrated into it. A lot of common problems remain the same across every industry vertical. Data management really does not change how you go and get access to the data. The second part is your model training—from pre-training to post-training to fine-tuning—and that flow is pretty much common across every industry. It's just the data and the kind of model which you are training that changes. Finally, you need to deploy this model into a production system, so you need to think about how you have the most efficient inference stack. There are many tools NVIDIA has developed, as well as our entire partner ecosystem, that can use it. There are many horizontally same capabilities which you need to know for taking this platform from one industry vertical to another. But if you go on top of that, take a customer care problem; whether you're talking about it in telcos or healthcare, of course there is a different knowledge base attached to that, but the flow is pretty much the same. There is a horizontal technology component and also some horizontal workflows which can be fine-tuned to your specific industry verticals. What I'm hearing is the foundation can be very similar and the foundation has to be solid, which is a good segue into the thing that powers all of this: CUDA. Stephen, you've been here almost as long as CUDA. CUDA is completing twenty years now, and it's fascinating to see it's still the most important piece of technology powering this whole revolution. So tell us, what does CUDA actually do? Yes, CUDA is the magic that connects your application to the GPU hardware. Anything that you are doing that is GPU accelerated, that involves accelerated computing, it goes through CUDA. In some ways, the secret of CUDA is that it's not one thing; it's many, many things. It's all these different layers, the kind of things that Pradeep was just talking about, where you can take pieces, mix and match them, and use the pieces you need while depending on all the other pieces just working with it. It's a deep platform stack, from frameworks at the top all the way down to the hardware controllers at the bottom. The magic is that whatever way you touch it, CUDA is the thing that is bringing you through to the underlying GPU hardware. You've obviously seen CUDA evolve over the last eighteen or twenty years. Give everyone a little background on how it started, some of the interesting stories, and your experiences. I wasn't there at the very beginning; I joined eighteen years ago when CUDA was already out in version 1.0. Ian Buck, who is the inventor of CUDA, was my boss, and he literally just said, "There's your computer, go and do something cool by Friday." That's how we inducted people into CUDA in those days. I had never touched CUDA, and I sat down and was like, "What's this thing all about?" It was very bare-bones. We had a compiler that barely worked and a couple of libraries. I was working on one of them, but we had no tools to debug things. In fact, in frustration, during that first week when I was trying to get my program to work, I asked, "How do you debug this?" They said, "You can't." So I actually wrote printf, which is the universal function that all programmers use to just print out data as they're running. I wrote printf for CUDA in my first week, and honestly, it's the best thing I've ever done for CUDA in the last twenty years. It's still super relevant; everybody uses it. That was the start of a journey of building to what is now hundreds of libraries across the CUDA-X platform. Pradeep, how long have you been here? I've been here almost fifteen plus years at NVIDIA. Tell us a little bit about your CUDA stories. Oh, my god. Stephen and I could spend the whole day just talking about that. The most important part is that I had the privilege of taking CUDA and CUDA-X libraries to a lot of our customers, really seeing where the rubber meets the road. I remember the reason you are trying to move to CUDA is to get performance, but during that process, you need to debug. For example, are you accessing the memory in a coalesced way or not? Are you having fundamental blockers like is your code compute-bound or memory-bound or latency-bound? There were no performance debuggers as you can imagine. One of our colleagues started a spreadsheet to measure all the counters, and we all started contributing to that Excel spreadsheet. It became our de facto tool for thinking through debugging performance for these applications. Very interestingly, in a few months, the spreadsheets had become so complex that even we were having a hard time understanding them. But that was a real beginning of how our visual profiler came into existence. I have a question from the audience: What is the difference between CUDA and CUDA libraries? A lot of times they get used interchangeably. You can think of CUDA as a platform, and CUDA-X libraries are something written on top of that. A simple way of thinking about CUDA-X libraries is that they are reusable pieces of IP or technology, or a lot of learnings and data patterns which we have observed over a number of years. We ensure this software is well-tuned and optimized for our architecture and we are committed to supporting it as long as we live. There are so many examples: cuBLAS, cuFFT, cuRAND for random number generators, Riva for natural language processing, and MONAI for medical imaging. There are probably more than 900 libraries, and many are built by our partners. I kind of think of it as a car; the whole machine is CUDA, and the libraries are the wheels. Nobody can use the car without the wheels, but you wouldn't reinvent the wheel either. If there's a library that serves your purpose, that's saving you time. That's tapping into the expertise of all the CUDA community and all the NVIDIA developers. It's a critical part of the whole that you depend on, but you then build your own stuff on top of it. I have an audience question: How do you get started with the CUDA libraries for a particular industry? You can go on developer.nvidia.com/cuda-x-libraries. That's where we list all our libraries which we have worked on in the last twenty plus years. This also takes you to a lot of domain-specific libraries as well as all of our partner and ecosystem libraries. It's fair to say that for enterprise customers starting out, a library would help them get to market or get to AI faster, right? There are hundreds of libraries at this point which form part of the whole CUDA ecosystem, and so there is almost certainly one or more things that are going to help you with the area that you need to develop in. The library gives you a starting point and lets you focus your energy and your development resources on your innovations. You find what is relevant to you, what can you use, what can you extend and enhance, and you go from there. One more audience question: CUDA is from NVIDIA and runs on NVIDIA GPUs. How can you call it open? A lot of people confuse that when we say open; open does not mean it's open source, and open does not mean it's vendor-neutral. When I say open, it means it's freely available; you can go take it and use it without asking for any permissions from NVIDIA. More interestingly, if you go and build a business on top of that, that's completely your IP and your business. We also follow open standards; for example, C++ has an open standard, so we follow that as part of what we call open CUDA. We still have a lot of open source; many of our CUDA-X libraries are open source, our Nemotron model is open source, and even our datasets are open. Open does not mean it has to run on every vendor's hardware; it means it's freely available and we will support it as long as we live. We work intensively with all the different open standards because I think that builds community interoperability and the common baselines that everybody depends on. NVIDIA submits a lot of code to PyTorch and various frameworks, and likewise, Meta submits things to some of our libraries. It really is a community with a lot of back-and-forth and cross-pollination. The openness there is critical so that we can all build on a common platform and all of the different developments that people do can be shared, or kept proprietary if it's your IP. It's fascinating how much appreciation there is in the developer community for CUDA. It's quite humbling to see the talent within NVIDIA and in the ecosystem taking this power and bringing innovation. CUDA is a platform where not only us, but the entire world, can do their life's work. What libraries are banks, hospitals, pharma, or manufacturing companies using? In finance, think of data processing; you can go and use cuDF. You can also use cuGraph because you need to create big graphs to figure out the context. In biopharma, we have BioNeMo and cuEquivariance. For smart cities, we have Metropolis. In retail, we have a Recommender System engine. I think about this whole stack of CUDA and how these pieces work together. NVIDIA's own libraries depend on other libraries; cuDNN depends on cuBLAS, which depends on CUTLASS, which is a library for tensor cores. Prior to the AI revolution, when GPU acceleration was getting into scientific computing, medical devices, and finance, all these components were building up. As the AI revolution shows up, these AI libraries are plugging into these classical computing mechanisms that people have built over the past twenty years. This interoperability means you're seeing people suddenly augment what they were doing with a completely new discipline. Stephen, how do you think about CUDA in terms of evolving generations of hardware, from Hopper to Blackwell to Rubin? I'm in the software organization as an architect of CUDA, but fully half my time is spent working with the hardware team. The key thing NVIDIA has is that it builds both the hardware and the software that runs on it. I spend a lot of time talking to the hardware people saying, "Here's something we want to enable," or they come up with an invention like Tensor Cores, and we figure out how to adapt the software to expose this. There's this very tight coupling and a long-term roadmap between both hardware and software co-evolving together. Because we have this designed evolution, it means that as the software advances, we can take those advancements and apply them even to older hardware. We still support the Ampere and Turing architectures. We release advancements as far back as we possibly can so that every GPU gets them, which comes from this core co-design. My agents are going to be unhappy if I don't talk about agentic AI. Let's look at the slide we discussed in episode three. In simpler ways, an agent is not that different than hiring a new coworker. Think about how a human thinks: they have a brain (the model), they take in context (what do you remember?), they observe (what do you see around you?), they reason, and then they act. Jensen at GTC Taipei announced that we are going to have our CUDA library as skills so that agents can leverage that. Every CUDA-X library will be available as a skill. For example, if you are processing a pandas pipeline, that agent can be exposed to the skill of our data processing library to do that efficiently. Or, if you are optimizing a CUDA kernel, that agent can be exposed to the skill of our visual profiler to figure out where the bottlenecks are. I think this is going to drive the adoption of these libraries an order of magnitude higher because billions of agents will run in the future and each one is going to call many skills to do the job. What does this do to a developer's job? I must admit that I haven't written much code this year because I now write prompts, because the agents write the code. Pretty much everyone I know in software is working the same way. Your agents are your team; they're very productive and you lead and guide them. By putting out skills for every single aspect of the CUDA-X universe, it means that agents can identify exactly what the right tool is for each job. Agents work very fast, and so these skills act as documentation that contains information on how and when to use something. It's a force multiplier; I can do three times more than I used to be able to do. Each one of us doesn't need to be a people manager; we all will become agent managers. It's important for each of us to understand certain domains in great depth because that skill will always be needed, and you can codify that as an agent skill. There is also a great need for thinking through the system design; the people who can think across the full stack are going to be more important than ever. I personally can't wait for my first one-on-one with my agents. I have some feedback for them. But this was a great conversation. To bring it all back, when we think about enterprise AI factories, easy is hard; making something seamless and effortless is extremely hard. We always have to keep our ultimate customers—the users, the agents, and the machines—in mind. Thank you both for being here, and thank you to all our listeners. If you liked it, please share it with your friends, and we'll see you soon.

What Would Make an AI Assistant Worth Paying For?

a16z(298.0K)·50:15·10.0K views·2026-09-29
Anish AcharyaGeneral Partner, a16z David PawlanCreator, Assistant Benchmark
TL;DR- **Rapid Market Explosion:**
Personal AI agents and consumer assistants have exploded in popularity over a four-week period, shifting from general productivity tools to proactive, invisible assistants capable of saving consumers time and money through tasks like travel management, finance, and...

TL;DR: Personal AI agents and consumer assistants have exploded in popularity over a four-week period, shifting from general productivity tools to proactive, invisible assistants capable of saving consumers time and money through tasks like travel management, finance, and shopping.

Landscape of Personal AI Agents

  • Rapid Market Explosion:
      • The consumer AI agent space has expanded rapidly, with 122 different solutions currently tracked across categories, including 64 generalized B2C consumer-facing agents.
      • Early milestones included *Poke* (launched September 8), *Open Claw* (late November), *Instinct*, *Grockbot*, *Muse*, and longtail options like *Caddy*, *Ali*, and *Pi*.
  • Assistant Bench Metrics:
      • Created by David Pawlan, *Assistant Bench* compares AI assistants on a one-shot prompt use-case basis across 16 dimensions.
      • Launched 16 days prior to the recording, the site surpassed 100,000 visitors and received outreach from nearly every founder in the space.
  • Top User Behaviors:
      • Despite travel being a viral point of conversation, travel is actually the fourth most talked-about use case because people do not travel every day.
      • The top three daily use cases are: (1) Daily admin and inbox cleaning, (2) Agent orchestration (how agents talk to each other), and (3) Development/coding on mobile. Finance ranks fourth as the most interesting but least practical category.

Proactivity, Trust, and the "Invisible Assistant"

  • The Efficiency Misconception:
      • General populations do not care about being 10% more efficient; the best employees (and agents) are invisible, doing work in the background and updating users only when necessary.
  • Cost-Saving Workflows:
      • The most successful proactive agents will focus on cost savings rather than cost-making—such as going through past receipts to file HSA reimbursements, hitting up airlines for travel credits when flight prices drop, or connecting a home Grockbot to a sprinkler system to drop water bills by 50%.
  • The Trust Boundary:
      • Proactiveness carries massive defensibility, but it is a fine line; crossing it once destroys user trust. While users accept proactive background actions that require no downstream work (e.g., drafting emails, flight credits), direct actions impacting the user (e.g., switching insurance providers) require explicit permission.

Interfaces, Hardware, and Audio

  • Multi-Surface Preferences:
      • Interaction surface preferences fragment by generation and use case (e.g., Gen Z preferring text threads, older demographics or visual planners preferring apps and visual dream boards).
      • iMessage occupies a privileged, highly personal space on the phone compared to app grids, though chat interfaces risk becoming cluttered if every second thread is an agent.
  • Hardware Form Factors:
      • While startups are experimenting with pendants and watch straps, form factors will likely be driven by personal preference like jewelry.
      • Anish Acharya suggests the Meta/Muse "Charm" hardware might serve less as a mass-adoption win and more as a real-world data collection device to map out the physical world for Zuckerberg's future metaverse.
      • Audio-only wearables and full-duplex voice models (such as ChatGPT Voice running on GPT live one, capable of seeing Gmail and calendar threads) represent a major magic moment for users whose hands are busy.

Multiplayer Dynamics and Social Interaction

  • Group Chat Integration:
      • Adding agents directly to active group chats often feels intrusive or diminishes social capital due to low-EQ chatter.
      • Emerging alternatives include *Doc* (by Shane Mack via XMTP/A16Z), which acts as a silent listener in group chats, takes notes, and independently pings users in separate threads when action items arise.
  • Social Indirection:
      • Carefully designed agents can act as a non-human layer of social indirection, helping organize conflict or provide feedback without emotional friction.

Agentic Commerce and Network Effects

  • Marketplace Friction & Resistance:
      • Platforms are pushing back against automation; Amazon blocked Muse to protect its ad revenue and impulse-shopping loops, while Resi began shutting down accounts using browser automation for restaurant reservations.
  • Supply-Constrained vs. Demand-Constrained Commerce:
      • Hot restaurant tables and other supply-constrained domains will require more sophisticated calculations (such as maximizing AOV/LTV or loyalty) or move toward bidding wars.
      • Conversely, demand-constrained markets could see agent-driven group buying or automated supply-side bidding to benefit the consumer.
  • Serendipity and Longtail Commerce:
      • Rather than buying standard mass-market goods, agents could source hyper-tailored items from the longtail (e.g., commissioning a grandmother in Nebraska to knit white socks via Etsy or direct text messaging).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • David Pawlan: The trajectory of the personal agent space will continue moving toward increased proactivity and hyper-specialization (such as narrow startups building experts tailored to specific micro-demographics like single mothers with children under three).
  • Anish Acharya: Browser-use infrastructure and token costs will experience exponential deflation, making advanced agent capabilities cheap enough for more products to offer for free.

Notable Quotes
